(5)

By Yoneda, every type
\Gamma.\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{\mu}\vdash A\ \mathsf{type}_{1}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{}\mathop{@}{n}}
can be seen as a morphism
\mathbf{y}(\Gamma)\Rightarrow\llbracket\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{\mu}\rrbracket^{*}\tau_{n}
. Postcomposition with
\mathbf{Mod}_{\mu}
gives a morphism
\mathbf{y}(\Gamma)\Rightarrow\mathcal{T}_{m}
, which constitutes the interpretation of the type
\Gamma\vdash\langle\mu\mid A\rangle\ \mathsf{type}_{1}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{}\mathop{@}{m}}
.

\mathbf{mod}_{\mu}
interprets the introduction rule in a similar fashion. Nevertheless, asking that this
square be a pullback is stronger than the elimination rule. In Section
7
we
shall see that states that
\mathbf{Mod}_{\mu}
is a
dependent right adjoint
.

Instead, we will model our elimination rule by a
lifting structure
. We phrase this definition
in the internal language of the presheaf topos
\mathbf{PSh}(\mathcal{C}[m])
, i.e. extensional type
theory.
5
5

5

This is derived from unpublished work by Jon Sterling, Daniel Gratzer, Carlo
Angiuli, and Lars Birkedal.
This has a serious technical advantage: as the definition is given in
an empty context, the given lifts are automatically natural.

{defi}

[Left lifting structure]

Given
\vdash A,I,B\ \mathsf{type}
, a family
b:B\vdash E[b]\ \mathsf{type}
and a section

a:A\vdash i[a]:I
, we define the type
\vdash i[-]\pitchfork E[-]\ \mathsf{type}
of

left lifting structures
for
i
with respect to
E
to be

\textstyle i[-]\pitchfork E[-]\triangleq\prod_{C:I\to B}\prod_{c:\prod_{a:A}E[C(i[a])]}\left\{j:\prod_{p:I}E[C(p)]\mid\forall a:A.\ j(i[a])=c(a)\right\}

Informally, left lifting structures provide
diagonal fillers
for the diagram

A
I
\sum_{b:B}E[b]
B
\langle C(i[-]),c\rangle
i[-]
\pi_{1}
C
j

Intuitively,
C:I\to B
is the
motive
of an elimination: we would like to prove
E[C(p)]

for all
p:I
. At the same time,
c:\prod_{a:A}E[C(i[a])]
is a given section that specifies
the desired computational behaviour of this elimination at the ‘special case’
A
. The left lifting
structure then provides a section
j
of
E[-]
defined on all of
I
. This section is above
C
,
and extends
c
. Note that these fillers are not necessarily unique. Moreover, they are
automatically
natural
: as all the types involved in this definition are closed, we are at
liberty to weaken the context.

This style of lifting structure is an essential ingredient in recent work on models of intensional
identity types. First, they play an important rôle in natural models:
[
Awo18
, Lemma
19]
shows that they precisely correspond to enriched left lifting properties in the
sense of categorical homotopy theory
[
Rie14
, §13]
. In fact, the above definition
given above is a word-for-word restatement in the internal language. Second, such lifting structures
are also central devices in internal presentations of models of cubical type theory, in particular
the recent work of
[
OP18
]
.

We can now approach this in a manner similar to intensional identity types in
op. cit
. Recall
that the elimination rule for
\langle\nu\mid A\rangle
is

\frac{\nu:\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{M}}(o,n)\quad\mu:\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{M}}(n,m)\quad\Gamma\ \mathsf{ctx}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{}\mathop{@}m}\quad\Gamma.\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{\mu}.\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{\nu}\vdash A\ \mathsf{type}_{1}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{}\mathop{@}{o}}\quad\Gamma.\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{\mu}\vdash M_{0}:\langle\nu\mid A\rangle{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{}\mathop{@}n}\quad\Gamma.(\mu\mid\langle\nu\mid A\rangle)\vdash B\ \mathsf{type}_{1}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{}\mathop{@}{m}}\quad\Gamma.(\mu\circ\nu\mid A)\vdash M_{1}:B[{{\uparrow}}.{\mathsf{mod}_{\nu}(\mathbf{v}_{0})}]{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{}\mathop{@}m}}{\Gamma\vdash\mathsf{let}_{\mu}\ \mathsf{mod}_{\nu}(\,\underline{\phantom{-}}\,)\leftarrow M_{0}\ \mathsf{in}\ M_{1}:B[{\mathsf{id}}.{M_{0}}]{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{}\mathop{@}m}}

First, we must remove the ‘implicit cut’ with
M_{0}
. We construct the substitution

\Gamma.(\mu\mid\langle\nu\mid A\rangle).(\mu\circ\nu\mid A[{\uparrow}.\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{\mu\circ\nu}])\vdash\sigma\triangleq{{\uparrow^{2}}}.{\mathbf{v}_{0}}:\Gamma.(\mu\circ\nu\mid A){\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{}\mathop{@}m}

It then suffices to construct the elimination rule

\frac{\nu:\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{M}}(o,n)\quad\mu:\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{M}}(n,m)\quad\Gamma\ \mathsf{ctx}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{}\mathop{@}m}\quad\Gamma.\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{\mu}.\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{\nu}\vdash A\ \mathsf{type}_{1}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{}\mathop{@}{o}}\quad\Gamma.(\mu\mid\langle\nu\mid A\rangle)\vdash B\ \mathsf{type}_{1}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{}\mathop{@}{m}}\quad\Gamma.(\mu\circ\nu\mid A)\vdash M_{1}:B[{{\uparrow}}.{\mathsf{mod}_{\nu}(\mathbf{v}_{0})}]{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{}\mathop{@}m}}{\Gamma.(\mu\mid\langle\nu\mid A\rangle)\vdash\mathsf{let}_{\mu}\ \mathsf{mod}_{\nu}(\,\underline{\phantom{-}}\,)\leftarrow\mathbf{v}_{0}\ \mathsf{in}\ M_{1}[\sigma]:B{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{}\mathop{@}m}}

because we can calculate that

\Gamma\vdash(\mathsf{let}_{\mu}\ \mathsf{mod}_{\nu}(\,\underline{\phantom{-}}\,)\leftarrow\mathbf{v}_{0}\ \mathsf{in}\ M_{1}[\sigma])[{\mathsf{id}}.{M_{0}}]=\mathsf{let}_{\mu}\ \mathsf{mod}_{\nu}(\,\underline{\phantom{-}}\,)\leftarrow M_{0}\ \mathsf{in}\ M_{1}:B[{\mathsf{id}}.{M_{0}}]{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{}\mathop{@}m}

We can rephrase this as the existence of a diagonal filler in the diagram

\mathbf{y}(\Gamma.(\mu\circ\nu\mid A))
\mathbf{y}(\Gamma.(\mu\mid\langle\nu\mid A\rangle))
\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}_{m}
\mathcal{T}_{m}
\tau_{m}
\mathbf{y}({{\uparrow}}.{\mathsf{mod}_{\nu}(\mathbf{v}_{0})})
\mathchoice{\left\lfloor\mkern-2.0mu{B}\mkern-2.0mu\right\rfloor}{\left\lfloor\mkern-2.0mu{B}\mkern-2.0mu\right\rfloor}{\lfloor B\rfloor}{\lfloor B\rfloor}
\mathchoice{\left\lfloor\mkern-2.0mu{M_{1}[\sigma]}\mkern-2.0mu\right\rfloor}{\left\lfloor\mkern-2.0mu{M_{1}[\sigma]}\mkern-2.0mu\right\rfloor}{\lfloor M_{1}[\sigma]\rfloor}{\lfloor M_{1}[\sigma]\rfloor}
\mathchoice{\left\lfloor\mkern-2.0mu{\mathsf{let}_{\mu}\ \mathsf{mod}_{\nu}(\,\underline{\phantom{-}}\,)\leftarrow\mathbf{v}_{0}\ \mathsf{in}\ M_{1}[\sigma]}\mkern-2.0mu\right\rfloor}{\left\lfloor\mkern-2.0mu{\mathsf{let}_{\mu}\ \mathsf{mod}_{\nu}(\,\underline{\phantom{-}}\,)\leftarrow\mathbf{v}_{0}\ \mathsf{in}\ M_{1}[\sigma]}\mkern-2.0mu\right\rfloor}{\lfloor\mathsf{let}_{\mu}\ \mathsf{mod}_{\nu}(\,\underline{\phantom{-}}\,)\leftarrow\mathbf{v}_{0}\ \mathsf{in}\ M_{1}[\sigma]\rfloor}{\lfloor\mathsf{let}_{\mu}\ \mathsf{mod}_{\nu}(\,\underline{\phantom{-}}\,)\leftarrow\mathbf{v}_{0}\ \mathsf{in}\ M_{1}[\sigma]\rfloor}

We can use a left lifting structure on a carefully chosen slice category to obtain such diagonal
fillers. The internal language approach still applies because of the well-known lemma stating that
the slice of a presheaf topos is also a presheaf topos, but over the corresponding category of
elements. In symbols, for any
P:\mathbf{PSh}(\mathcal{C})
we have an equivalence
\mathbf{PSh}(\mathcal{C})/P\simeq\mathbf{PSh}(\int_{\mathcal{C}}P)
: see
[
MLM92
, III Ex. 8]

First, given
\nu:\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{M}}(o,n)
we construct the following pullback:

\llbracket\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{\nu}\rrbracket^{*}\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}_{o}
M
\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}_{n}
\llbracket\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{\nu}\rrbracket^{*}\mathcal{T}_{o}
\mathcal{T}_{n}
m
h
\llbracket\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{\nu}\rrbracket^{*}\tau_{o}
\mathbf{mod}_{\nu}
\mathbf{Mod}_{\nu}
\tau_{n}

The outer commuting square is that given by the formation and introduction for
\langle\nu\mid-\rangle
, as
in (
5
). Intuitively,
M
is a ‘generic
\nu
-modal terms object’ that
consists of terms
\Gamma\vdash M:\langle\nu\mid A\rangle{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{}\mathop{@}n}
, where

\Gamma.\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{\nu}\vdash A\ \mathsf{type}_{1}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{}\mathop{@}{o}}
. We know that
\llbracket\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{\mu}\rrbracket^{*}
has a left adjoint,
so it preserves pullbacks. Applying it to this diagram yields

\llbracket\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{\mu\circ\nu}\rrbracket^{*}\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}_{o}
\llbracket\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{\mu}\rrbracket^{*}M
\llbracket\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{\mu}\rrbracket^{*}\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}_{n}
\llbracket\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{\mu\circ\nu}\rrbracket^{*}\mathcal{T}_{o}
\llbracket\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{\mu}\rrbracket^{*}\mathcal{T}_{n}
\llbracket\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{\mu}\rrbracket^{*}m
\llbracket\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{\mu}\rrbracket^{*}h
\llbracket\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{\mu\circ\nu}\rrbracket^{*}\tau_{o}
\llbracket\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{\mu}\rrbracket^{*}\mathbf{mod}_{\nu}
\llbracket\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{\mu}\rrbracket^{*}\mathbf{Mod}_{\nu}
\llbracket\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{\mu}\rrbracket^{*}\tau_{n}

(6)

We have also used the fact that
(-)^{*}
is functorial to contract the two locks into
one. Moreover, we get that the unique mediating morphism is indeed
\llbracket\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{\mu}\rrbracket^{*}m
.

From this point onwards we will also work in the slice
\mathbf{PSh}(\mathcal{C}[m])/Z
, where
Z\triangleq\llbracket\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{\mu\circ\nu}\rrbracket^{*}\mathcal{T}_{o}
. In order to model the elimination rule
we will ask for a left lifting structure
in the slice category
, of type

\vdash\mathbf{open}_{\nu}^{\mu}:\llbracket\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{\mu}\rrbracket^{*}m\pitchfork Z^{*}(\tau_{m})

(7)

where both of these are considered as morphisms in the slice
\mathbf{PSh}(\mathcal{C}[m])/Z
,
respectively of type

\displaystyle\llbracket\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{\mu}\rrbracket^{*}m:\

\displaystyle\llbracket\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{\mu\circ\nu}\rrbracket^{*}\tau_{o}\to\llbracket\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{\mu}\rrbracket^{*}h

\displaystyle Z^{*}(\tau_{m}):\

\displaystyle Z^{*}(\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}_{m})\to Z^{*}(\mathcal{T}_{m})

Following
[
Awo18
]
we may calculate that this models the rule. We suppose its
premises, and construct the diagram of Figure
10
.

\mathbf{y}(\Gamma.(\mu\circ\nu\mid A))
\mathbf{y}(\Gamma.(\mu\mid\langle\nu\mid A\rangle))
\llbracket\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{\mu\circ\nu}\rrbracket^{*}\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}_{o}
\llbracket\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{\mu}\rrbracket^{*}M

\llbracket\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{\mu\circ\nu}\rrbracket^{*}\mathcal{T}_{o}

\mathchoice{\left\lfloor\mkern-2.0mu{\mathbf{q}}\mkern-2.0mu\right\rfloor}{\left\lfloor\mkern-2.0mu{\mathbf{q}}\mkern-2.0mu\right\rfloor}{\lfloor\mathbf{q}\rfloor}{\lfloor\mathbf{q}\rfloor}

\mathchoice{\left\lfloor\mkern-2.0mu{\langle\nu\mid A\rangle}\mkern-2.0mu\right\rfloor}{\left\lfloor\mkern-2.0mu{\langle\nu\mid A\rangle}\mkern-2.0mu\right\rfloor}{\lfloor\langle\nu\mid A\rangle\rfloor}{\lfloor\langle\nu\mid A\rangle\rfloor}

\llbracket\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{\mu}\rrbracket^{*}\mathbf{Mod}_{\nu}
\mathchoice{\left\lfloor\mkern-2.0mu{\mathsf{mod}_{\nu}(\mathbf{q})}\mkern-2.0mu\right\rfloor}{\left\lfloor\mkern-2.0mu{\mathsf{mod}_{\nu}(\mathbf{q})}\mkern-2.0mu\right\rfloor}{\lfloor\mathsf{mod}_{\nu}(\mathbf{q})\rfloor}{\lfloor\mathsf{mod}_{\nu}(\mathbf{q})\rfloor}

\mathchoice{\left\lfloor\mkern-2.0mu{\mathbf{q}}\mkern-2.0mu\right\rfloor}{\left\lfloor\mkern-2.0mu{\mathbf{q}}\mkern-2.0mu\right\rfloor}{\lfloor\mathbf{q}\rfloor}{\lfloor\mathbf{q}\rfloor}
\llbracket\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{\mu\circ\nu}\rrbracket^{*}\tau_{o}

Figure 10.
Modelling the elimination rule

The right (both top and bottom) part of the diagram is just (
6
). The
bottom composite is easily seen to correspond to the application of the introduction rule of

\langle\nu\mid-\rangle
to the type
\Gamma.\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{\mu}.\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{\nu}\vdash A\ \mathsf{type}_{1}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{}\mathop{@}{o}}
, and hence to the type

\Gamma.\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{\mu}\vdash\langle\nu\mid A\rangle\ \mathsf{type}_{1}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{}\mathop{@}{n}}
. The outer bottom square is the natural model pullback
square that defines the object
\Gamma.(\mu\mid\langle\nu\mid A\rangle)
, and we thus get a mediating morphism
to
\llbracket\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{\mu}\rrbracket^{*}M
, and that the bottom-left square is also a pullback. The left (both
top and bottom) part of the diagram is the natural model pullback square that defines the object

\Gamma.(\mu\circ\nu\mid A)
. We hence get a mediating morphism

{\mathbf{p}}.{\mathbf{mod}_{\nu}(\mathbf{q})}:\Gamma.(\mu\circ\nu\mid A)\to\Gamma.(\mu\mid\langle\nu\mid A\rangle)
. Finally, for the same reasons as the bottom composite, the top
composite is easily seen to correspond to the term
\mathsf{mod}_{\nu}(\mathbf{q})
.

We write
\sum_{Z}:\mathbf{PSh}(\mathcal{C}[m])/Z\to\mathbf{PSh}(\mathcal{C}[m])
for the usual domain
projection functor, so that
\sum_{Z}\dashv Z^{*}
. Now, using the usual approach to slice
categories—where the cartesian product
\times_{Z}
is the pullback—we see from the diagram that

\displaystyle\sum_{Z}(\mathchoice{\left\lfloor\mkern-2.0mu{A}\mkern-2.0mu\right\rfloor}{\left\lfloor\mkern-2.0mu{A}\mkern-2.0mu\right\rfloor}{\lfloor A\rfloor}{\lfloor A\rfloor}\times_{Z}\llbracket\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{\mu\circ\nu}\rrbracket^{*}\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}_{o})

\displaystyle\cong\mathbf{y}(\Gamma.(\mu\circ\nu\mid A))

(8)

\displaystyle\sum_{Z}(\mathchoice{\left\lfloor\mkern-2.0mu{A}\mkern-2.0mu\right\rfloor}{\left\lfloor\mkern-2.0mu{A}\mkern-2.0mu\right\rfloor}{\lfloor A\rfloor}{\lfloor A\rfloor}\times_{Z}\llbracket\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{\mu}\rrbracket^{*}h)

\displaystyle\cong\mathbf{y}(\Gamma.(\mu\mid\langle\nu\mid A\rangle))

\displaystyle\sum_{Z}(\mathsf{id}_{\mathchoice{\left\lfloor\mkern-2.0mu{A}\mkern-2.0mu\right\rfloor}{\left\lfloor\mkern-2.0mu{A}\mkern-2.0mu\right\rfloor}{\lfloor A\rfloor}{\lfloor A\rfloor}}\times_{Z}\llbracket\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{\mu}\rrbracket^{*}m)

\mathbf{y}(\Gamma.(\mu\circ\nu\mid A))

\mathbf{open}_{\nu}^{\mu}

We may then use the lifting structure to prove a diagonal filler, and transpose backwards along the
adjunction to obtain a filler for (
9
). The naturality of all these steps
(composing isomorphisms, transposition, and lifting structure) ensure that the choice is natural.

5.2.3.
Boolean Structure

A boolean structure is defined similarly to the structure for modal types. First, we require
two constants, as well as naturally given diagonal fillers for the appropriate squares:

1
\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}_{m}
1
\mathcal{T}_{m}
\mathbf{tt}
\mathbf{ff}
\mathbf{Bool}
\tau_{m}

1+1
\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}_{m}
\tau_{m}^{-1}\left(\mathbf{Bool}\right)
\mathcal{T}_{m}
[\mathbf{tt},\mathbf{ff}]
\tau_{m}

\tau_{m}^{-1}\left(\mathbf{Bool}\right)
is the fibre of
\tau_{m}
over
\mathbf{Bool}
, and the map
[\mathbf{tt},\mathbf{ff}]
is
obtained as the cotuple of the maps obtained by factoring
\mathbf{tt}
and
\mathbf{ff}
through the fibre.
Requiring a left lifting structure

\mathbf{if}:\left[\mathbf{tt},\mathbf{ff}\right]\pitchfork\tau_{m}[-]

in the internal language provides enough naturality to yield diagonal fillers for all squares

\mathbf{y}(\Gamma)+\mathbf{y}(\Gamma)
\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}_{m}
\mathbf{y}(\Gamma.\mathbf{Bool})
\mathcal{T}_{m}
[{\mathsf{id}}.{\mathbf{tt}},{\mathsf{id}}.{\mathbf{ff}}]
\tau_{m}

5.2.4.
Universe

The universe itself is given by a presheaf
\mathcal{S}_{m}
at each mode
m
. The Coquand-style
isomorphism is implemented by a natural transformation
\mathbf{Uni}:1\Rightarrow\tau_{m}
, which stands for the
universe type at mode
m
, as well as a natural isomorphism

\tau_{m}^{-1}\left(\mathbf{Uni}\right)\cong\mathcal{S}_{m}

As the pullback of
\tau_{m}
along
\mathbf{Uni}
is
\mathbf{y}(1.\mathbf{Uni})
, this exactly postulates an
isomorphism between terms of the universe and small types. The coercion from small to large type is
interpreted by a natural transformation
\mathbf{lift}:\mathcal{S}_{m}\Rightarrow\mathcal{T}_{m}
that maps each small
type to its associated large type. Moreover, we ask that the formation rules
factor
through
small types: we require a mediating morphism in each of the following diagrams:
6
6

6

There are
also similar diagrams for
\Sigma
and intensional identity types.

\mathbf{P}_{\llbracket\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{\mu}\rrbracket^{*}\tau_{n}}(\mathcal{S}_{m})
\mathcal{S}_{m}
\mathbf{P}_{\llbracket\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{\mu}\rrbracket^{*}\tau_{n}}(\mathcal{T}_{m})
\mathcal{T}_{m}
\mathbf{lift}
{\textstyle\prod}

\llbracket\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{\mu}\rrbracket^{*}\mathcal{S}_{n}
\mathcal{S}_{m}
\llbracket\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{\mu}\rrbracket^{*}\mathcal{T}_{n}
\mathcal{T}_{m}
\mathbf{lift}
\mathbf{Mod}_{\mu}

1
\mathcal{S}_{m}
1
\mathcal{T}_{m}
\mathbf{lift}
\mathbf{Bool}

These factorisations ensure that type formation is closed under small types, and commutation ensures
that the coercions commute with the type formers defintitionally.

5.2.5.
The full definition

We have shown how to interpret each rule of
MTT
through natural models. In fact, every step of
our working is reversible: each contraption we have introduced precisely corresponds to the portion
of the generalized algebraic theory it was used to interpret. In summary, we can make the following
definition.

{defi}

A model of
MTT
over
\mathcal{M}
consists of

•

a modal context structure for
\mathcal{M}
(as in
Definition
5.1.1
), and a

•

a modal natural model on that context structure (as in
Definition
5.1.2
)

such that the modal natural model supports

•

dependent product types

•

dependent sum types (at each mode)

•

intensional identity types (at each mode)

•

modal types

•

a boolean type (at each mode), and

•

a universe of small types.

5.3.
Morphisms of Models

The generalized algebraic theory (GAT) of
MTT
also induces a notion of morphism between
models. Traditionally neglected, morphisms are of paramount importance when one produces semantic
proofs of metatheoretic properties, such as canonicity, a proof of which we will present in
Section
6
.

The last decade has seen much use of relatively weak morphisms of CwFs, i.e. morphisms which preserve
structures only up to isomorphism: see e.g.
[
CD14
,
BCM+20
]
. However, our
proof of canonicity will require the strictest notion of CwF morphism, i.e. a GAT homomorphism. Such
morphisms preserve
all
structure on-the-nose, including context extension, and were
introduced alongside CwFs by
[
Dyb96
]
. As we are using natural models, we will use an
adaptation due to
[
New18
, §2.3]
. We believe that one may construct a biequivalence
or biadjunction between a category based on strict morphisms and one based on weaker ones, as done
by e.g.
[
Uem19
]
, but we will leave that to future work.

{defi}

[Strict morphism of natural models]

A morphism of natural models

\mathchoice{\left\lfloor\mkern-2.0mu{A}\mkern-2.0mu\right\rfloor}{\left\lfloor\mkern-2.0mu{A}\mkern-2.0mu\right\rfloor}{\lfloor A\rfloor}{\lfloor A\rfloor}:\mathbf{y}(\Gamma)\Rightarrow\mathcal{T}_{c}

The first component comes from a natural transformation
\mathbf{P}_{\tau_{c}}(-)\Rightarrow\mathbf{P}_{F^{*}\mathcal{T}_{d}}(-)
, which exists because (

) not only

. A more conceptual proof is given by

, Cor. 3.14]
in the

We then require that all connectives (
{\textstyle\prod}
,
{\textstyle\sum}
,
\mathbf{refl}
) strictly commute with these
morphisms. Finally, we can extend this to a model of
MTT
by requiring not just a functor, but a
natural transformation
\mathcal{C}\Rightarrow\mathcal{D}
, where
\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D}:{\mathcal{M}}^{\mathsf{coop}}\to\mathbf{Cat}
satisfy the obvious generalizations of the conditions written above. Specifying this formally:

{defi}

A morphism between two models of
MTT
,
\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D}
, is given by a 2-natural
transformation
\alpha:\mathcal{C}\Rightarrow\mathcal{D}
. Moreover, we require a choice of commuting
squares:

\widetilde{\mathcal{U}}_{\mathcal{C}[m]}
\alpha_{m}^{*}\widetilde{\mathcal{U}}_{\mathcal{D}[m]}
\mathcal{U}_{\mathcal{C}[m]}
\alpha^{*}\mathcal{U}_{\mathcal{D}[m]}
\tau_{\mathcal{C}[m]}
\widetilde{\varphi}_{m}
\varphi_{m}
\alpha^{*}\tau_{\mathcal{D}[m]}

Moreover, we require that
(\varphi,\widetilde{\varphi})
strictly commutes with all operations.

\displaystyle\alpha_{m}(\Gamma.(\mu\mid A))

\displaystyle=\alpha_{m}(\Gamma).(\mu\mid\varphi(A))

\displaystyle{\textstyle\prod}\circ(\varphi,\varphi)

\displaystyle=\varphi\circ{\textstyle\prod}

\displaystyle\mathbf{lam}\circ(\varphi,\widetilde{\varphi})

\displaystyle=\widetilde{\varphi}\circ\mathbf{lam}

\displaystyle{\textstyle\sum}\circ(\varphi,\varphi)

\displaystyle=\varphi\circ{\textstyle\sum}

\displaystyle\mathbf{pair}\circ(\varphi,\widetilde{\varphi})

\displaystyle=\widetilde{\varphi}\circ\mathbf{pair}

\displaystyle\mathbf{Mod}_{\mu}\circ\llbracket\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{\mu}\rrbracket^{*}\varphi

\displaystyle=\varphi\circ\mathbf{Mod}_{\mu}

\displaystyle\mathbf{mod}_{\mu}\circ\llbracket\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{\mu}\rrbracket^{*}\widetilde{\varphi}

\displaystyle=\widetilde{\varphi}\circ\mathbf{mod}_{\mu}

\displaystyle\mathbf{open}_{\mu}^{\nu}\circ(\widetilde{\varphi},\llbracket\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{\mu}\rrbracket^{*}\widetilde{\varphi})

\displaystyle=\widetilde{\varphi}\circ\mathbf{open}_{\mu}^{\nu}

\displaystyle\mathbf{Bool}

\displaystyle=\varphi\circ\mathbf{Bool}

\displaystyle\mathbf{tt}=\widetilde{\varphi}\circ\mathbf{tt}

\displaystyle\quad\mathbf{ff}=\widetilde{\varphi}\circ\mathbf{ff}

\displaystyle\mathbf{if}\circ(\widetilde{\varphi},\widetilde{\varphi},\widetilde{\varphi})

\displaystyle=\widetilde{\varphi}\circ\mathbf{if}

\displaystyle\mathbf{Id}\circ(\varphi,\widetilde{\varphi},\widetilde{\varphi})

\displaystyle=\varphi\circ\mathbf{Id}

\displaystyle\mathbf{refl}\circ\widetilde{\varphi}

\displaystyle=\widetilde{\varphi}\circ\mathbf{refl}

\displaystyle\mathbf{J}\circ(\widetilde{\varphi},\widetilde{\varphi})

\displaystyle=\widetilde{\varphi}\circ\mathbf{J}

Remark 1

(The Initiality of Syntax)
.

Under this definition of homomorphism, we immediately have an initial
model
[
Car78
,
KKA19
]
. We will
define
this model to be our
syntax and designate it
(\mathbb{S}[m])_{m\in\mathcal{M}}
.

6.
Canonicity

Equipped with the generalized algebraic theory of Section
4
and its reformulation
through natural models in Section
5
, we are ready to show that the syntax of
MTT
is
well-behaved. In this section we will sketch the main parts of a proof of
canonicity
for

MTT
. This is a basic well-behavedness property which guarantees that terms of ground type, e.g.
\mathbb{B}
, can be normalized. As expected, the proof is independent of the mode theory.

Proposition 2
.

If
\vdash M:\mathbb{B}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{}\mathop{@}m}
, then either
\vdash M=\mathsf{tt}:\mathbb{B}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{}\mathop{@}m}
or

\vdash M=\mathsf{ff}:\mathbb{B}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{}\mathop{@}m}
.

This kind of result would traditionally be established by producing a rewriting system along with a
lengthy PER model construction. We will instead opt for a proof given by constructing a
glued

model
[
Coq19
,
KHS19
]
. The contexts, types, and terms of this model will
contain a syntactic component (resp. a context, type, or term), along with a
proof-relevant
predicate
that is appropriately fibred over it. The base types of this model are carefully chosen
so that a normal form can be extracted from proofs of the predicate. By interpreting a term of
ground type in the glued model we automatically obtain a proof of the predicate, from which we
extract a normal form.

Such proofs involve two steps: defining the glued construction, and proving that it is a model.
While the first step is often straightforward, the second usually involves checking innumerable
equations. In order to shorten the proof sketched here we will make a simplifying assumption (effectively adding an equation to the algebraic syntax): we
will assume that locks preserve the empty context, i.e. that

\cdot.\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{\mu}=\cdot\ \mathsf{ctx}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{}\mathop{@}{m}}

for
\mu:\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{M}}(m,n)
. Using the universal property of the terminal context, this implies

\cdot.\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{\mu}\vdash\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.85}{$\displaystyle\text{\faIconFromMacro{faKey}}$}}{\scalebox{0.85}{$\textstyle\text{\faIconFromMacro{faKey}}$}}{\scalebox{0.85}{$\scriptstyle\text{\faIconFromMacro{faKey}}$}}{\scalebox{0.85}{$\scriptscriptstyle\text{\faIconFromMacro{faKey}}$}}_{\cdot}^{\alpha}=\cdot=\mathsf{id}:\cdot.\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{\nu}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{}\mathop{@}m}

(12)

Requiring this equation unfortunately limits our class of models to those where the left adjoint
strictly preserves the terminal product. Despite this simplification the proof remains rather long,
so we will only sketch the construction of the modal natural models. The missing details may be
found in an accompanying technical report.

Remark 3
.

In what follows we will assume the existence of two Grothendieck universes
\mathcal{V}^{\prime}\subset\mathcal{V}:\mathbf{Set}
. We could make do with just one but at the price of some contortions, which are both
unnecessary and tiresome. We will assume that the sets of contexts, substitutions, types, and
terms of the syntactic model are
\mathcal{V}^{\prime}
-small.

6.1.
The Glued Model

We begin by defining the context structure.

{defi}

[Glued Contexts]
A glued context
\Gamma
at mode
m
consists of a context
\Gamma^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.90}{$\displaystyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\textstyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptstyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptscriptstyle\lhd$}}}\in\mathsf{ctx}_{m}
, a predicate

\Gamma^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.90}{$\displaystyle\RHD$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\textstyle\RHD$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptstyle\RHD$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptscriptstyle\RHD$}}}\in\mathcal{V}
, and a function

\phi_{\Gamma}:\Gamma^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.90}{$\displaystyle\RHD$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\textstyle\RHD$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptstyle\RHD$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptscriptstyle\RHD$}}}\to\mathsf{sb}_{m}(\cdot,\Gamma^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.90}{$\displaystyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\textstyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptstyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptscriptstyle\lhd$}}})

A glued context
\Gamma=(\Gamma^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.90}{$\displaystyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\textstyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptstyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptscriptstyle\lhd$}}},\Gamma^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.90}{$\displaystyle\RHD$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\textstyle\RHD$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptstyle\RHD$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptscriptstyle\RHD$}}})
can be thought of as a proof-relevant
predicate over substitutions into the syntactic context
\Gamma^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.90}{$\displaystyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\textstyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptstyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptscriptstyle\lhd$}}}
. An element
x\in\Gamma^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.90}{$\displaystyle\RHD$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\textstyle\RHD$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptstyle\RHD$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptscriptstyle\RHD$}}}
can be thought of as a proof that the predicate holds of the substitution

\phi_{\Gamma}(x):\cdot\to\Gamma^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.90}{$\displaystyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\textstyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptstyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptscriptstyle\lhd$}}}
. We will henceforth use the metavariable
\Gamma
to
range over
glued contexts
, and denote contexts of the syntax by
\Gamma^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.90}{$\displaystyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\textstyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptstyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptscriptstyle\lhd$}}}
.

{defi}

[Glued Substitutions]
A glued substitution from
\Delta
to
\Gamma
at mode
m
is a pair of a substitution

\gamma^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.90}{$\displaystyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\textstyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptstyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptscriptstyle\lhd$}}}\in\mathsf{sb}_{m}(\Delta^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.90}{$\displaystyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\textstyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptstyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptscriptstyle\lhd$}}},\Gamma^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.90}{$\displaystyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\textstyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptstyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptscriptstyle\lhd$}}})
and a function
\gamma^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.90}{$\displaystyle\RHD$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\textstyle\RHD$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptstyle\RHD$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptscriptstyle\RHD$}}}:\Delta^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.90}{$\displaystyle\RHD$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\textstyle\RHD$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptstyle\RHD$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptscriptstyle\RHD$}}}\to\Gamma^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.90}{$\displaystyle\RHD$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\textstyle\RHD$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptstyle\RHD$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptscriptstyle\RHD$}}}
such that

\forall x\in\Delta^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.90}{$\displaystyle\RHD$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\textstyle\RHD$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptstyle\RHD$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptscriptstyle\RHD$}}}.\ \phi_{\Gamma}(\gamma^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.90}{$\displaystyle\RHD$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\textstyle\RHD$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptstyle\RHD$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptscriptstyle\RHD$}}}(x))=\gamma^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.90}{$\displaystyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\textstyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptstyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptscriptstyle\lhd$}}}\circ\phi_{\Delta}(x):\cdot\to\Gamma^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.90}{$\displaystyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\textstyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptstyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptscriptstyle\lhd$}}}

Glued contexts and glued substitutions form a category, viz. the comma category

\mathcal{C}[m]\triangleq(1_{\mathcal{V}}\downarrow\mathsf{sb}_{m}(\cdot,-))

which we take as the category of contexts at mode
m
. Next, we define a 2-functor from
\mathcal{M}

sending each
m
to
\mathcal{C}[m]
. For each
\mu:\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{M}}(m,n)
a functor

\llbracket\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{\mu}\rrbracket:\mathcal{C}[n]\to\mathcal{C}[m]
as by taking the
\mathcal{C}[n]
morphism

\Delta^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.90}{$\displaystyle\RHD$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\textstyle\RHD$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptstyle\RHD$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptscriptstyle\RHD$}}}
\mathsf{sb}_{n}(\cdot,\Delta^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.90}{$\displaystyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\textstyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptstyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptscriptstyle\lhd$}}})
\Gamma^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.90}{$\displaystyle\RHD$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\textstyle\RHD$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptstyle\RHD$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptscriptstyle\RHD$}}}
\mathsf{sb}_{n}(\cdot,\Gamma^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.90}{$\displaystyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\textstyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptstyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptscriptstyle\lhd$}}})
\gamma^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.90}{$\displaystyle\RHD$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\textstyle\RHD$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptstyle\RHD$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptscriptstyle\RHD$}}}
\gamma^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.90}{$\displaystyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\textstyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptstyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptscriptstyle\lhd$}}}\circ-
\phi_{\Delta}
\phi_{\Gamma}

to the
\mathcal{C}[m]
morphism:

\Delta^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.90}{$\displaystyle\RHD$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\textstyle\RHD$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptstyle\RHD$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptscriptstyle\RHD$}}}
\mathsf{sb}_{m}(\cdot,\Delta^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.90}{$\displaystyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\textstyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptstyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptscriptstyle\lhd$}}}.\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{\mu})
\Gamma^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.90}{$\displaystyle\RHD$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\textstyle\RHD$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptstyle\RHD$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptscriptstyle\RHD$}}}
\mathsf{sb}_{m}(\cdot,\Gamma^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.90}{$\displaystyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\textstyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptstyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptscriptstyle\lhd$}}}.\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{\mu})
\gamma^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.90}{$\displaystyle\RHD$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\textstyle\RHD$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptstyle\RHD$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptscriptstyle\RHD$}}}
\gamma^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.90}{$\displaystyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\textstyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptstyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptscriptstyle\lhd$}}}.\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{\mu}\circ-
\phi_{\Delta.\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{\mu}}
\phi_{\Gamma.\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{\mu}}

where the function
\phi_{\Delta.\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{\mu}}
is defined by

\phi_{\Delta.\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{\mu}}(x)\triangleq\phi_{\Delta}(x).\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{\mu}\ :\ \cdot\to\Delta^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.90}{$\displaystyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\textstyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptstyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptscriptstyle\lhd$}}}.\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{\mu}

Notice that the equation
\cdot.\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{\mu}=\cdot
is necessary to ensure that this definition is
well-typed. The diagram commutes because locks act functorially on substitutions. It is also
functorial in
\mu
, because
\Gamma.\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{\mu}.\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{\nu}=\Gamma.\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{\mu\circ\nu}
, and

\Gamma.\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{1}=\Gamma
.

We define a 2-cell
\llbracket\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{\mu}\rrbracket\Rightarrow\llbracket\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{\nu}\rrbracket
for each
\alpha:\nu\Rightarrow\mu
. The
component at
(\Gamma^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.90}{$\displaystyle\RHD$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\textstyle\RHD$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptstyle\RHD$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptscriptstyle\RHD$}}},\Gamma^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.90}{$\displaystyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\textstyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptstyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptscriptstyle\lhd$}}},\phi_{\Gamma})
is

\Gamma^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.90}{$\displaystyle\RHD$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\textstyle\RHD$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptstyle\RHD$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptscriptstyle\RHD$}}}
\mathsf{sb}_{m}(\cdot,\Gamma^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.90}{$\displaystyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\textstyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptstyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptscriptstyle\lhd$}}}.\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{\mu})
\Gamma^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.90}{$\displaystyle\RHD$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\textstyle\RHD$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptstyle\RHD$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptscriptstyle\RHD$}}}
\mathsf{sb}_{m}(\cdot,\Gamma^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.90}{$\displaystyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\textstyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptstyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptscriptstyle\lhd$}}}.\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{\nu})
\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.85}{$\displaystyle\text{\faIconFromMacro{faKey}}$}}{\scalebox{0.85}{$\textstyle\text{\faIconFromMacro{faKey}}$}}{\scalebox{0.85}{$\scriptstyle\text{\faIconFromMacro{faKey}}$}}{\scalebox{0.85}{$\scriptscriptstyle\text{\faIconFromMacro{faKey}}$}}_{\Gamma^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.90}{$\displaystyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\textstyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptstyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptscriptstyle\lhd$}}}}^{\alpha}\circ-
\phi_{\Gamma.\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{\mu}}
\phi_{\Gamma.\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{\nu}}

This diagram commutes because of (
12
), so it is a morphism in comma
category. Naturality follows from the numerous equations pertaining to keys and their composition.

This completes the definition of a strict 2-functor
{\mathcal{M}}^{\mathsf{coop}}\to\mathbf{Cat}_{1}
as per
Section
5.1.1
. Next, we must define the modal natural model structure for
each category of contexts.

Remark 4
.

For the rest of this section we will freely use type-theoretic notation, viewing the predicate

\Gamma^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.90}{$\displaystyle\RHD$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\textstyle\RHD$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptstyle\RHD$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptscriptstyle\RHD$}}}\to\mathsf{sb}_{m}(\cdot,\Gamma^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.90}{$\displaystyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\textstyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptstyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptscriptstyle\lhd$}}})
as a family fibred over
\mathsf{sb}_{m}(\cdot,\Gamma^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.90}{$\displaystyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\textstyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptstyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptscriptstyle\lhd$}}})
, i.e. a map
\mathsf{sb}_{m}(\cdot,\Gamma^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.90}{$\displaystyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\textstyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptstyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptscriptstyle\lhd$}}})\to\mathcal{V}
.

We will follow the convention that symbols annotated with
(-)^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.90}{$\displaystyle\RHD$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\textstyle\RHD$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptstyle\RHD$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptscriptstyle\RHD$}}}
correspond to proof-relevant
constructions—i.e. members of the predicate, or maps between predicates—whereas symbols
annotated with
(-)^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.90}{$\displaystyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\textstyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptstyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptscriptstyle\lhd$}}}
correspond to pieces of syntax (e.g. terms, contexts, substitutions). In
particular,
\gamma^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.90}{$\displaystyle\RHD$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\textstyle\RHD$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptstyle\RHD$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptscriptstyle\RHD$}}}
will not necessarily refer to a fibred map between proof-relevant
predicates, but also to a generalized element of
\Gamma^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.90}{$\displaystyle\RHD$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\textstyle\RHD$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptstyle\RHD$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptscriptstyle\RHD$}}}
.

In other words, when
\gamma^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.90}{$\displaystyle\RHD$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\textstyle\RHD$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptstyle\RHD$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptscriptstyle\RHD$}}}\in\Gamma^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.90}{$\displaystyle\RHD$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\textstyle\RHD$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptstyle\RHD$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptscriptstyle\RHD$}}}
and
\phi_{\Gamma}(\gamma^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.90}{$\displaystyle\RHD$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\textstyle\RHD$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptstyle\RHD$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptscriptstyle\RHD$}}})=\gamma^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.90}{$\displaystyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\textstyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptstyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptscriptstyle\lhd$}}}\ :\ \cdot\to\Gamma^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.90}{$\displaystyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\textstyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptstyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptscriptstyle\lhd$}}}
, we will abusively write
\gamma^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.90}{$\displaystyle\RHD$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\textstyle\RHD$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptstyle\RHD$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptscriptstyle\RHD$}}}:\Gamma^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.90}{$\displaystyle\RHD$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\textstyle\RHD$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptstyle\RHD$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptscriptstyle\RHD$}}}(\gamma^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.90}{$\displaystyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\textstyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptstyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptscriptstyle\lhd$}}})
. That is, we will view
\gamma^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.90}{$\displaystyle\RHD$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\textstyle\RHD$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptstyle\RHD$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptscriptstyle\RHD$}}}
as living in the fibre of

\phi_{\Gamma}
over
\gamma^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.90}{$\displaystyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\textstyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptstyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptscriptstyle\lhd$}}}
. This amounts to considering
\gamma^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.90}{$\displaystyle\RHD$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\textstyle\RHD$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptstyle\RHD$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptscriptstyle\RHD$}}}
as a
proof

that the predicate
\Gamma^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.90}{$\displaystyle\RHD$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\textstyle\RHD$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptstyle\RHD$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptscriptstyle\RHD$}}}
holds at the substitution
\gamma^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.90}{$\displaystyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\textstyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptstyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptscriptstyle\lhd$}}}
. Observe that if

\gamma^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.90}{$\displaystyle\RHD$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\textstyle\RHD$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptstyle\RHD$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptscriptstyle\RHD$}}}:\Gamma.\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{\mu}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.90}{$\displaystyle\RHD$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\textstyle\RHD$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptstyle\RHD$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptscriptstyle\RHD$}}}(\gamma^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.90}{$\displaystyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\textstyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptstyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptscriptstyle\lhd$}}})
then
\gamma^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.90}{$\displaystyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\textstyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptstyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptscriptstyle\lhd$}}}
must be of the form

\theta^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.90}{$\displaystyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\textstyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptstyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptscriptstyle\lhd$}}}.\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{\mu}
for some
\theta^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.90}{$\displaystyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\textstyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptstyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptscriptstyle\lhd$}}}\ :\ \cdot\to\Gamma^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.90}{$\displaystyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\textstyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptstyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptscriptstyle\lhd$}}}
with
\gamma^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.90}{$\displaystyle\RHD$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\textstyle\RHD$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptstyle\RHD$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptscriptstyle\RHD$}}}:\Gamma^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.90}{$\displaystyle\RHD$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\textstyle\RHD$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptstyle\RHD$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptscriptstyle\RHD$}}}(\theta^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.90}{$\displaystyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\textstyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptstyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptscriptstyle\lhd$}}})
.

Types over
\mathcal{C}[m]
are given by the presheaf

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}_{m}(\Gamma)\triangleq\{

\displaystyle\quad A^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.90}{$\displaystyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\textstyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptstyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptscriptstyle\lhd$}}}\in\mathsf{type}_{m}^{1}(\Gamma^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.90}{$\displaystyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\textstyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptstyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptscriptstyle\lhd$}}});

\displaystyle\quad A^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.90}{$\displaystyle\RHD$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\textstyle\RHD$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptstyle\RHD$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptscriptstyle\RHD$}}}:(\gamma^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.90}{$\displaystyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\textstyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptstyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptscriptstyle\lhd$}}}:\mathsf{sb}_{m}(\cdot,\Gamma^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.90}{$\displaystyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\textstyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptstyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptscriptstyle\lhd$}}}))\to(\gamma^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.90}{$\displaystyle\RHD$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\textstyle\RHD$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptstyle\RHD$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptscriptstyle\RHD$}}}:\Gamma^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.90}{$\displaystyle\RHD$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\textstyle\RHD$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptstyle\RHD$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptscriptstyle\RHD$}}}(\gamma^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.90}{$\displaystyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\textstyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptstyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptscriptstyle\lhd$}}}))\to\mathsf{tm}_{m}(\cdot,A^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.90}{$\displaystyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\textstyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptstyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptscriptstyle\lhd$}}}[\gamma^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.90}{$\displaystyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\textstyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptstyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptscriptstyle\lhd$}}}])\to\mathcal{V}

\displaystyle\}

Extending this presheaf with some additional data gives a presheaf of terms over
\mathcal{C}[m]
:

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}_{m}(\Gamma)\triangleq\{

\displaystyle\quad A^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.90}{$\displaystyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\textstyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptstyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptscriptstyle\lhd$}}}\in\mathsf{type}_{m}^{1}(\Gamma^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.90}{$\displaystyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\textstyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptstyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptscriptstyle\lhd$}}});

\displaystyle\quad A^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.90}{$\displaystyle\RHD$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\textstyle\RHD$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptstyle\RHD$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptscriptstyle\RHD$}}}:(\gamma^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.90}{$\displaystyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\textstyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptstyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptscriptstyle\lhd$}}}:\mathsf{sb}_{m}(\cdot,\Gamma^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.90}{$\displaystyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\textstyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptstyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptscriptstyle\lhd$}}}))\to(\gamma^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.90}{$\displaystyle\RHD$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\textstyle\RHD$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptstyle\RHD$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptscriptstyle\RHD$}}}:\Gamma^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.90}{$\displaystyle\RHD$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\textstyle\RHD$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptstyle\RHD$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptscriptstyle\RHD$}}}(\gamma^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.90}{$\displaystyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\textstyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptstyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptscriptstyle\lhd$}}}))\to\mathsf{tm}_{m}(\cdot,A^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.90}{$\displaystyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\textstyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptstyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptscriptstyle\lhd$}}}[\gamma^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.90}{$\displaystyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\textstyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptstyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptscriptstyle\lhd$}}}])\to\mathcal{V}

\displaystyle\quad M^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.90}{$\displaystyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\textstyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptstyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptscriptstyle\lhd$}}}\in\mathsf{tm}_{m}(\Gamma^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.90}{$\displaystyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\textstyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptstyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptscriptstyle\lhd$}}},A^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.90}{$\displaystyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\textstyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptstyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptscriptstyle\lhd$}}});

\displaystyle\quad M^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.90}{$\displaystyle\RHD$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\textstyle\RHD$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptstyle\RHD$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptscriptstyle\RHD$}}}:(\gamma^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.90}{$\displaystyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\textstyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptstyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptscriptstyle\lhd$}}}:\mathsf{sb}_{m}(\cdot,\Gamma^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.90}{$\displaystyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\textstyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptstyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptscriptstyle\lhd$}}}))\to(\gamma^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.90}{$\displaystyle\RHD$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\textstyle\RHD$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptstyle\RHD$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptscriptstyle\RHD$}}}:\Gamma^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.90}{$\displaystyle\RHD$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\textstyle\RHD$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptstyle\RHD$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptscriptstyle\RHD$}}}(\gamma^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.90}{$\displaystyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\textstyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptstyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptscriptstyle\lhd$}}}))\to A^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.90}{$\displaystyle\RHD$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\textstyle\RHD$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptstyle\RHD$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptscriptstyle\RHD$}}}(\gamma^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.90}{$\displaystyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\textstyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptstyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptscriptstyle\lhd$}}},\gamma^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.90}{$\displaystyle\RHD$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\textstyle\RHD$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptstyle\RHD$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptscriptstyle\RHD$}}},M^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.90}{$\displaystyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\textstyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptstyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptscriptstyle\lhd$}}}[\gamma^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.90}{$\displaystyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\textstyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptstyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptscriptstyle\lhd$}}}])

\displaystyle\}

Thus, a
type
over
\Gamma=(\Gamma^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.90}{$\displaystyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\textstyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptstyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptscriptstyle\lhd$}}},\varphi_{\Gamma})
in the glued model consists of a
type
\Gamma^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.90}{$\displaystyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\textstyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptstyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptscriptstyle\lhd$}}}\vdash A^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.90}{$\displaystyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\textstyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptstyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptscriptstyle\lhd$}}}\ \mathsf{type}_{1}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{}\mathop{@}{m}}
of
MTT
, along with another predicate, a family of

\mathcal{V}
-small sets, indexed over both closing substitutions
\gamma^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.90}{$\displaystyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\textstyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptstyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptscriptstyle\lhd$}}}
that satisfy the predicate

\Gamma^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.90}{$\displaystyle\RHD$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\textstyle\RHD$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptstyle\RHD$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptscriptstyle\RHD$}}}
and terms of type
A^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.90}{$\displaystyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\textstyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptstyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptscriptstyle\lhd$}}}[\Gamma^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.90}{$\displaystyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\textstyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptstyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptscriptstyle\lhd$}}}]
.

A
term
over
\Gamma
in the glued model adds to the above a term

\Gamma^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.90}{$\displaystyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\textstyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptstyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptscriptstyle\lhd$}}}\vdash M^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.90}{$\displaystyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\textstyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptstyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptscriptstyle\lhd$}}}:A^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.90}{$\displaystyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\textstyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptstyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptscriptstyle\lhd$}}}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{}\mathop{@}m}
of that type, and a section
M^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.90}{$\displaystyle\RHD$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\textstyle\RHD$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptstyle\RHD$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptscriptstyle\RHD$}}}
of the aforementioned
predicate. This section produces a proof that the predicate holds at that term after we close it by
applying any substitution
\gamma^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.90}{$\displaystyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\textstyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptstyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptscriptstyle\lhd$}}}
of which the
\Gamma^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.90}{$\displaystyle\RHD$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\textstyle\RHD$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptstyle\RHD$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptscriptstyle\RHD$}}}
holds. The reindexing action of
these presheaves is defined by the action of substitution on contexts, types, and terms of
MTT
.

It can be shown that the projection
\tau_{m}(\Gamma)\triangleq(A^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.90}{$\displaystyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\textstyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptstyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptscriptstyle\lhd$}}},A^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.90}{$\displaystyle\RHD$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\textstyle\RHD$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptstyle\RHD$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptscriptstyle\RHD$}}},M^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.90}{$\displaystyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\textstyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptstyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptscriptstyle\lhd$}}},M^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.90}{$\displaystyle\RHD$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\textstyle\RHD$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptstyle\RHD$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptscriptstyle\RHD$}}})\mapsto(A^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.90}{$\displaystyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\textstyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptstyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptscriptstyle\lhd$}}},A^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.90}{$\displaystyle\RHD$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\textstyle\RHD$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptstyle\RHD$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptscriptstyle\RHD$}}})
that maps terms to types by forgetting the additional data defines a
representable natural transformation in the sense of Section
5.1.2
; the full
proof can be found in the technical report. With respect to the connectives, we only show how to
interpret the base type
\mathbb{B}
, as per Section
5.2.3
. For the formation and
introduction rules we define:

\displaystyle\mathbf{Bool}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.90}{$\displaystyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\textstyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptstyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptscriptstyle\lhd$}}}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{B}

\displaystyle\mathbf{Bool}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.90}{$\displaystyle\RHD$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\textstyle\RHD$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptstyle\RHD$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptscriptstyle\RHD$}}}

\displaystyle=\lambda\gamma^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.90}{$\displaystyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\textstyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptstyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptscriptstyle\lhd$}}},\gamma^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.90}{$\displaystyle\RHD$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\textstyle\RHD$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptstyle\RHD$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptscriptstyle\RHD$}}},M^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.90}{$\displaystyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\textstyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptstyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptscriptstyle\lhd$}}}.\ (M^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.90}{$\displaystyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\textstyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptstyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptscriptstyle\lhd$}}}[\gamma^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.90}{$\displaystyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\textstyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptstyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptscriptstyle\lhd$}}}]=\mathsf{tt})+(M^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.90}{$\displaystyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\textstyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptstyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptscriptstyle\lhd$}}}[\gamma^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.90}{$\displaystyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\textstyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptstyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptscriptstyle\lhd$}}}]=\mathsf{ff})

\displaystyle\mathbf{tt}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.90}{$\displaystyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\textstyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptstyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptscriptstyle\lhd$}}}

\displaystyle=\mathsf{tt}

\displaystyle\mathbf{tt}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.90}{$\displaystyle\RHD$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\textstyle\RHD$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptstyle\RHD$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptscriptstyle\RHD$}}}

\displaystyle=\lambda\_.\ \iota_{0}(\star)

\displaystyle\mathbf{ff}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.90}{$\displaystyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\textstyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptstyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptscriptstyle\lhd$}}}

\displaystyle=\mathsf{ff}

\displaystyle\mathbf{ff}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.90}{$\displaystyle\RHD$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\textstyle\RHD$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptstyle\RHD$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptscriptstyle\RHD$}}}

\displaystyle=\lambda\_.\ \iota_{1}(\star)

We must now define the left lifting structure
\mathbf{if}:[\mathbf{tt},\mathbf{ff}]\pitchfork\tau_{m}
. In
type-theoretic notation:

\displaystyle\mathbf{if}(C,[c_{0},c_{1}],M)^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.90}{$\displaystyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\textstyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptstyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptscriptstyle\lhd$}}}

\displaystyle=\mathsf{if}(C^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.90}{$\displaystyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\textstyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptstyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptscriptstyle\lhd$}}};c_{0}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.90}{$\displaystyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\textstyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptstyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptscriptstyle\lhd$}}};c_{1}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.90}{$\displaystyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\textstyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptstyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptscriptstyle\lhd$}}};M^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.90}{$\displaystyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\textstyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptstyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptscriptstyle\lhd$}}})

\displaystyle\mathbf{if}(C,[c_{0},c_{1}],M)^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.90}{$\displaystyle\RHD$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\textstyle\RHD$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptstyle\RHD$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptscriptstyle\RHD$}}}

\displaystyle=\lambda\gamma^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.90}{$\displaystyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\textstyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptstyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptscriptstyle\lhd$}}},\gamma^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.90}{$\displaystyle\RHD$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\textstyle\RHD$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptstyle\RHD$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptscriptstyle\RHD$}}}.\ \begin{cases}c_{0}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.90}{$\displaystyle\RHD$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\textstyle\RHD$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptstyle\RHD$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptscriptstyle\RHD$}}}(\gamma^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.90}{$\displaystyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\textstyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptstyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptscriptstyle\lhd$}}},\gamma^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.90}{$\displaystyle\RHD$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\textstyle\RHD$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptstyle\RHD$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptscriptstyle\RHD$}}})&\text{if $M^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.90}{$\displaystyle\RHD$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\textstyle\RHD$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptstyle\RHD$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptscriptstyle\RHD$}}}(\gamma^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.90}{$\displaystyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\textstyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptstyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptscriptstyle\lhd$}}},\gamma^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.90}{$\displaystyle\RHD$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\textstyle\RHD$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptstyle\RHD$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptscriptstyle\RHD$}}})=\iota_{0}(\star)$}\\
c_{1}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.90}{$\displaystyle\RHD$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\textstyle\RHD$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptstyle\RHD$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptscriptstyle\RHD$}}}(\gamma^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.90}{$\displaystyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\textstyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptstyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptscriptstyle\lhd$}}},\gamma^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.90}{$\displaystyle\RHD$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\textstyle\RHD$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptstyle\RHD$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptscriptstyle\RHD$}}})&\text{if $M^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.90}{$\displaystyle\RHD$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\textstyle\RHD$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptstyle\RHD$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptscriptstyle\RHD$}}}(\gamma^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.90}{$\displaystyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\textstyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptstyle\lhd$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptscriptstyle\lhd$}}},\gamma^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.90}{$\displaystyle\RHD$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\textstyle\RHD$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptstyle\RHD$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptscriptstyle\RHD$}}})=\iota_{1}(\star)$}\end{cases}

6.2.
Deriving Canonicity

With the gluing model constructed, the rest of the proof is surprisingly easy and boils down to one
fact, which is immediate by inspection:

Theorem 5
.

The 2-natural transformation
\pi:\mathcal{C}[-]\Rightarrow\mathbb{S}[-]
from the glued model to the
syntactic model which forgets the predicates extends to a morphism of models.

Thus, assuming
\cdot.\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{\mu}=\cdot\ \mathsf{ctx}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{}\mathop{@}{m}}
it follows that

Corollary 6
.

For any closed term
\vdash M:A{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{}\mathop{@}m}
, there is a witness for
\llbracket A\rrbracket^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.90}{$\displaystyle\RHD$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\textstyle\RHD$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptstyle\RHD$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptscriptstyle\RHD$}}}(M)
.

Proof 6.1
.

By Theorem
5
and initiality we must have
\pi(\llbracket M\rrbracket)=M
, and
so
\llbracket M\rrbracket^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.90}{$\displaystyle\RHD$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\textstyle\RHD$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptstyle\RHD$}}{\scalebox{0.90}{$\scriptscriptstyle\RHD$}}}
is a witness.

Theorem 7

(Closed Term Canonicity)
.

If
\cdot\vdash M:A{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{}\mathop{@}m}
is a closed term, then

•

If
A=\mathbb{B}
then
\cdot\vdash M=\mathsf{tt}:\mathbb{B}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{}\mathop{@}m}
or

\cdot\vdash M=\mathsf{ff}:\mathbb{B}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{}\mathop{@}m}
.

•

If
A=\mathsf{Id}_{A_{0}}(N_{0},N_{1})
then
\cdot\vdash N_{0}=N_{1}:A_{0}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{}\mathop{@}m}
and

\cdot\vdash M=\mathsf{refl}(N_{0}):\mathsf{Id}_{A_{0}}(N_{0},N_{1}){\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{}\mathop{@}m}
.

•

If
A=\langle\nu\mid A_{0}\rangle
then there is an
\cdot\vdash N:A_{0}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{}\mathop{@}n}
such that

\cdot\vdash M=\mathsf{mod}_{\nu}(N):\langle\nu\mid A_{0}\rangle{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{}\mathop{@}m}
.

Proof 6.2
.

Immediate by Corollary
6
and the definition of the semantic predicates
at
\mathbb{B}
,
\mathsf{Id}_{A_{0}}(N_{0},N_{1})
, and
\langle\nu\mid A_{0}\rangle
respectively.

7.
Dependent Right Adjoints

Over the past couple of years the structure of a
dependent right adjoint (DRA)
has arisen as
a natural notion of dependent modality in Martin-Löf type theory. In this section we will study
the relationship between
MTT
modalities and DRAs in detail. After reviewing the definition of a
DRA, we will prove that a suitably functorial collection of DRAs induces a model of
MTT
. As
mentioned before, this implies that
MTT
modalities are weaker than DRAs. Following that, we will
investigate sufficient conditions for extending an ordinary right adjoint to a DRA.

7.1.
Dependent right adjoints in natural models

A dependent right adjoint
7
7

7

DRAs were introduced by
[
BCM+20
]
as
endomodalities, but we generalise them to multiple modes.

is an adaptation of the notion of adjunction to the dependent setting: instead of acting
on objects of the context category, the ‘right adjoint’ only acts on types and terms.

Given a pair of natural models
(\mathcal{D},\tau_{\mathcal{D}})
and
(\mathcal{C},\tau_{\mathcal{C}})
, a DRA from the second to the
first comprises a functor
L:\mathcal{D}\to\mathcal{C}
between the underlying context categories, as well as a
pullback diagram of the following shape in
\mathbf{PSh}(\mathcal{D})
:

L^{*}\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}_{\mathcal{C}}
\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}_{\mathcal{D}}
L^{*}\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{C}}
\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{D}}
L^{*}\tau_{\mathcal{C}}
r
R
\tau_{\mathcal{D}}

R
is the action on types, and
r
is the action on terms. Note that, while the ‘left adjoint’
L

acts on context categories, the ‘right adjoint’
(R,r)
only acts on types and terms. The fact the
square is a pullback amounts to requiring a multimode generalization of the definition given by

[
BCM+20
]
. Intuitively, for each term
\Gamma\vdash M:R(A)
, the pullback square
gives a unique term
L(\Gamma)\vdash N:A
such that
\Gamma\vdash M=r(N):R(A)
. If we wish
the modality to preserve the size of types, we must also require a
R^{\prime}
such that

L^{*}\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{C}}
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{D}}
L^{*}\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{C}}
\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{D}}
R^{\prime}
R
\mathbf{lift}

7.2.
DRAs as Models of
MTT

We will now show that DRAs can be used to construct models of
MTT
. As a consequence,
MTT

modalities are slightly weaker than DRAs.

Theorem 8
.

Suppose that we have

•

for each
m\in\mathcal{M}
a natural model
(\mathcal{C}[m],\hbox to32.68pt{\vbox to13.44pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 7.65222pt\lower-1.5pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{

{}{}}}{
{}{}}

\lxSVG@closescope }}}

.

Proof 7.1
.

Define a 2-functor
{\mathcal{M}}^{\mathsf{coop}}\to\mathbf{Cat}
by
m\mapsto\mathcal{C}[m]
,
\mu\mapsto\llbracket\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{\mu}\rrbracket
, and
\alpha\mapsto\llbracket\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.85}{$\displaystyle\text{\faIconFromMacro{faKey}}$}}{\scalebox{0.85}{$\textstyle\text{\faIconFromMacro{faKey}}$}}{\scalebox{0.85}{$\scriptstyle\text{\faIconFromMacro{faKey}}$}}{\scalebox{0.85}{$\scriptscriptstyle\text{\faIconFromMacro{faKey}}$}}^{\alpha}\rrbracket
. We must show how to define context extension, and how to interpret the connectives. As before, we only show the modal cases, the others being straightforward.

Modal Context Extension:

For each type
\mathbf{y}(\Gamma)

\llbracket\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{\mu}\rrbracket^{*}\mathcal{T}_{n}
\scriptstyle\mathchoice{\left\lfloor\mkern-2.0mu{A}\mkern-2.0mu\right\rfloor}{\left\lfloor\mkern-2.0mu{A}\mkern-2.0mu\right\rfloor}{\lfloor A\rfloor}{\lfloor A\rfloor}
we need a pullback

\mathbf{y}(\Gamma^{\prime})
\llbracket\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{\mu}\rrbracket^{*}\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}_{n}
\mathbf{y}(\Gamma)
\llbracket\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{\mu}\rrbracket^{*}\mathcal{T}_{n}
\mathbf{y}(p^{\prime})
\mathchoice{\left\lfloor\mkern-2.0mu{q^{\prime}}\mkern-2.0mu\right\rfloor}{\left\lfloor\mkern-2.0mu{q^{\prime}}\mkern-2.0mu\right\rfloor}{\lfloor q^{\prime}\rfloor}{\lfloor q^{\prime}\rfloor}
\mathchoice{\left\lfloor\mkern-2.0mu{A}\mkern-2.0mu\right\rfloor}{\left\lfloor\mkern-2.0mu{A}\mkern-2.0mu\right\rfloor}{\lfloor A\rfloor}{\lfloor A\rfloor}
\llbracket\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{\mu}\rrbracket^{*}\tau_{n}

Write
\mathchoice{\left\lfloor\mkern-2.0mu{\mathbf{Mod}_{\mu}(A)}\mkern-2.0mu\right\rfloor}{\left\lfloor\mkern-2.0mu{\mathbf{Mod}_{\mu}(A)}\mkern-2.0mu\right\rfloor}{\lfloor\mathbf{Mod}_{\mu}(A)\rfloor}{\lfloor\mathbf{Mod}_{\mu}(A)\rfloor}\triangleq\mathbf{Mod}_{\mu}\circ\mathchoice{\left\lfloor\mkern-2.0mu{A}\mkern-2.0mu\right\rfloor}{\left\lfloor\mkern-2.0mu{A}\mkern-2.0mu\right\rfloor}{\lfloor A\rfloor}{\lfloor A\rfloor}
.
\tau_{m}
is a natural model, so form
the pullback square for
\Gamma^{\prime}\triangleq\Gamma.\mathbf{Mod}_{\mu}(A)
. Pasting this with the DRA
pullback for
\mathbf{Mod}_{\mu}
forms

\mathbf{y}(\Gamma.\mathbf{Mod}_{\mu}(A))
\mathbf{y}(\Gamma)
\llbracket\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{\mu}\rrbracket^{*}\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}_{n}
\llbracket\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{\mu}\rrbracket^{*}\mathcal{T}_{n}
\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}_{m}
\mathcal{T}_{m}
\mathbf{y}(\mathbf{p})
\widehat{\mathchoice{\left\lfloor\mkern-2.0mu{\mathbf{q}}\mkern-2.0mu\right\rfloor}{\left\lfloor\mkern-2.0mu{\mathbf{q}}\mkern-2.0mu\right\rfloor}{\lfloor\mathbf{q}\rfloor}{\lfloor\mathbf{q}\rfloor}}
\mathchoice{\left\lfloor\mkern-2.0mu{A}\mkern-2.0mu\right\rfloor}{\left\lfloor\mkern-2.0mu{A}\mkern-2.0mu\right\rfloor}{\lfloor A\rfloor}{\lfloor A\rfloor}
\llbracket\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{\mu}\rrbracket^{*}\tau_{n}
\mathbf{mod}_{\mu}
\mathbf{Mod}_{\mu}
\tau_{m}

(13)

As the outer square commutes, we can fill in the dotted arrow. By the pullback lemma, the square
on the left is a pullback too. Letting
\Gamma.(\mu\mid A)\triangleq\Gamma.\mathbf{Mod}_{\mu}(A)

proves that
\left(\tau_{m}\right)_{m\in\mathcal{M}}
is a modal natural model.

Modal Types:

This is the heart of the proof. First, we need a commuting square

\llbracket\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{\mu}\rrbracket^{*}\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}_{n}
\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}_{m}
\llbracket\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{\mu}\rrbracket^{*}\mathcal{T}_{n}
\mathcal{T}_{m}
\mathbf{mod}_{\mu}
\mathbf{Mod}_{\mu}
\tau_{m}

(14)

Such a square is given as part of a DRA by definition, and is in fact a pullback!

To model the elimination rule, recall the definition of the object
M
used in Section
5.2.2
:

\llbracket\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{\mu\circ\nu}\rrbracket^{*}\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}_{o}
\llbracket\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{\mu}\rrbracket^{*}M
\llbracket\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{\mu}\rrbracket^{*}\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}_{n}
\llbracket\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{\mu\circ\nu}\rrbracket^{*}\mathcal{T}_{o}
\llbracket\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{\mu}\rrbracket^{*}\mathcal{T}_{n}
\llbracket\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{\mu}\rrbracket^{*}m
\llbracket\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{\mu\circ\nu}\rrbracket^{*}\tau_{o}
\llbracket\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{\mu}\rrbracket^{*}\mathbf{mod}_{\nu}
\llbracket\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{\mu}\rrbracket^{*}\mathbf{Mod}_{\nu}
\llbracket\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{\mu}\rrbracket^{*}\tau_{n}

As
\llbracket\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{\mu}\rrbracket^{*}
preserves pullbacks, the outer square is a pullback too. Hence

\llbracket\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{\mu}\rrbracket^{*}m
must be an isomorphism. The elimination rule requires a
left-lifting structure:

\vdash\mathbf{open}_{\nu}^{\mu}:\left(\llbracket\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{\mu}\rrbracket^{*}m\right)\pitchfork(\llbracket\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{\mu\circ\nu}\rrbracket^{*}\mathcal{T}_{o})^{*}(\tau_{m}[-])

Using the inverse of
\llbracket\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{\mu}\rrbracket^{*}m
we can construct this by

\mathbf{open}_{\nu}^{\mu}\triangleq\lambda C.\ \lambda c.\ c\circ\llbracket\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{\mu}\rrbracket^{*}(m^{-1})

\prod
Structure:

Equipping each
\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}_{m}

\mathcal{T}_{m}
\scriptstyle\tau_{m}
with a modal
\prod
structure is relatively
straightforward to do in the internal language; intuitively, the reason is the isomorphism

(\llbracket\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{\mu}\rrbracket^{*}\tau_{n})^{-1}\left(A\right)\cong\tau_{m}^{-1}\left(\mathbf{Mod}_{\mu}{A}\right)

which is derived from the fact
\Gamma.(\mu\mid A)\triangleq\Gamma.\mathbf{Mod}_{\mu}{A}
(where
the first dot is the defined context extension, and the second dot is given by the natural
model). However, we can also prove it in a more abstract way: we paste together the two pullback
squares

\mathbf{P}_{\llbracket\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{\mu}\rrbracket^{*}\tau_{n}}(\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}_{m})
\mathbf{P}_{\llbracket\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{\mu}\rrbracket^{*}\tau_{n}}(\mathcal{T}_{m})
\mathbf{P}_{\tau_{m}}(\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}_{m})
\mathbf{P}_{\tau_{m}}(\mathcal{T}_{m})
\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}_{m}
\mathcal{T}_{m}
\tau_{m}
\phi_{\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}_{m}}
\phi_{\mathcal{T}_{m}}
\mathbf{P}_{\llbracket\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{\mu}\rrbracket^{*}\tau_{n}}(\tau_{m})
\mathbf{P}_{\tau_{m}}(\tau_{m})
\mathbf{lam}
{\textstyle\prod}

The square on the right is the pullback that interprets
{\textstyle\prod}
in the natural model
\tau_{m}
.
The square on the left is a naturality square of the natural transformation

\phi:\mathbf{P}_{\llbracket\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{\mu}\rrbracket^{*}\tau_{n}}(-)\Rightarrow\mathbf{P}_{\tau_{m}}(-)

which exists because the pullback square (
14
) defines a morphism of
polynomials. Moreover, the naturality squares of
\phi
are cartesian: see
[
New18
, §§1.2.16–1.2.18]
.

This theorem is a particularly flexible tool, as many modalities naturally form DRAs, and it is
easier to check the DRA conditions than
MTT
model conditions as summarized in Definition

5.2.5
. As a first example of this flexibility we show that it leads to an
almost immediate proof of consistency.

Corollary 9
.

No matter what the mode theory is, there is no term
\cdot\vdash M:\mathsf{Id}_{\mathbb{B}}(\mathsf{tt},\mathsf{ff}){\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{}\mathop{@}m}
.
In other words,
MTT
is consistent.

Proof 7.2
.

Suppose that we have a model of
MLTT
with one universe in some category
\mathcal{C}
. We may construct
a functor
{\mathcal{M}}^{\mathsf{coop}}\to\mathbf{Cat}
by sending every mode to
\mathcal{C}
, and everything else to the
identity. This is stricty 2-functorial, and each identity functor is a DRA. Hence, by
Theorem
8
there is a model of
MTT
in which each mode is interpreted by

\mathcal{C}
. Therefore, if a term
M:\mathsf{Id}_{\mathbb{B}}(\mathsf{tt},\mathsf{ff})
were definable in
MTT
, we would
have a term of that type in every model of
MLTT
. But
MLTT
itself is consistent: see

[
Coq19
]
for a short proof.

7.3.
DRAs from right adjoints

Having established that a series of models of
MLTT
related by DRAs can be used to interpret

MTT
, we now turn to the problem of constructing those DRAs themselves. We shall prove a lemma
that allows us to lift any well-behaved right adjoint to a DRA. Versions of this result have
appeared before, both in the paper on DRAs
[
BCM+20
, Lemma 17]
, and in a technical
report By the third author
[
Nuy18
, Prop. 2.1.4]
.

In Section
5.3
we discussed the notion of a
strict
morphism of natural
models. Using the same notation we define the following weaker notion.

{defi}

A
weak morphism of natural models

(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{T}_{c})\to(\mathcal{D},\mathcal{T}_{d})
consists of a functor

F:\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{D}
, and a commuting square

\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}_{c}
F^{*}\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}_{d}
\mathcal{T}_{c}
F^{*}\mathcal{T}_{d}
\tau_{c}
\widetilde{\varphi}
\varphi
F^{*}\tau_{d}

such that
F(1)=1
and the canonical morphism
F(\Gamma.A)\to F\Gamma.\varphi(A)
is an isomorphism. We say that this morphism
preserves size

whenever there is a commuting square

\mathcal{S}_{c}
F^{*}\mathcal{S}_{d}
\mathcal{T}_{c}
F^{*}\mathcal{T}_{d}
\mathbf{lift}
\mathcal{S}_{\varphi}
\varphi
F^{*}\mathbf{lift}

This kind of morphism can also be found in the thesis of
[
New18
, §§2.3.9]
. We are
interested in it because it captures exactly the necessary good behaviour which is required to
extend a right adjoint to act on types and terms.

Lemma 10
.

Suppose that
(\mathcal{C},\tau_{\mathcal{C}})
and
(\mathcal{D},\tau_{\mathcal{D}})
are natural models, and that
L\dashv R

is an adjunction between
\mathcal{C}
and
\mathcal{D}
. If the right adjoint
R:\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{D}
extends to a weak
morphism of natural models then it gives rise to a dependent right adjoint. Moreover, the
resulting DRA is size-preserving whenever
R
is.

Proof 7.3
.

We first fix some notation: we write
\eta:\textsf{Id}\Rightarrow RL
for the unit of the
adjunction
L\dashv R
. Moreover, we assume a commuting square

\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}_{\mathcal{C}}
R^{*}\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}_{\mathcal{D}}
\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{C}}
R^{*}\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{D}}
\tau_{\mathcal{C}}
r
R
R^{*}\tau_{\mathcal{D}}

(15)

that witnesses the weak natural model morphism structure of
R
, and write

\nu_{\Gamma,A}:R\Gamma.\textsf{R}(A)\xrightarrow{\cong}R(\Gamma.A)

for the canonical isomorphism corresponding to
\mathchoice{\left\lfloor\mkern-2.0mu{A}\mkern-2.0mu\right\rfloor}{\left\lfloor\mkern-2.0mu{A}\mkern-2.0mu\right\rfloor}{\lfloor A\rfloor}{\lfloor A\rfloor}:\mathbf{y}(\Gamma)\Rightarrow\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{C}}
.

We construct the DRA by first applying the weak morphism
R
to a dependent type over a
context of the form
L(\Delta)
, and then pulling that back along the unit of the adjunction.
Diagrammatically, we define the square

L^{*}\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}_{\mathcal{C}}
L^{*}\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{C}}
L^{*}R^{*}\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}_{\mathcal{D}}
L^{*}R^{*}\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{D}}
\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}_{\mathcal{D}}
\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{D}}
L^{*}\tau_{\mathcal{C}}
L^{*}\textsf{r}
L^{*}\textsf{R}
\tau_{\mathcal{D}}
L^{*}R^{*}\tau_{\mathcal{D}}
\eta^{*}_{\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}_{\mathcal{D}}}
\eta^{*}_{\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{D}}}

The left part is the image of
(
15
) under the
L^{*}
functor, and the
right part is a naturality square for the natural transformation
\eta^{*}:L^{*}R^{*}=(RL)^{*}\Rightarrow\textsf{Id}
induced by the unit.

To show that this is a DRA we must show that this is a pullback, and it suffices to do so on the representables. Assume
we have
\mathchoice{\left\lfloor\mkern-2.0mu{A}\mkern-2.0mu\right\rfloor}{\left\lfloor\mkern-2.0mu{A}\mkern-2.0mu\right\rfloor}{\lfloor A\rfloor}{\lfloor A\rfloor}:\mathbf{y}(\Delta)\Rightarrow L^{*}\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{C}}
and a
\mathchoice{\left\lfloor\mkern-2.0mu{M}\mkern-2.0mu\right\rfloor}{\left\lfloor\mkern-2.0mu{M}\mkern-2.0mu\right\rfloor}{\lfloor M\rfloor}{\lfloor M\rfloor}:\mathbf{y}(\Delta)\Rightarrow\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}_{\mathcal{D}}
such that the diagram commutes. Switching to type-theoretic notation, this
amounts to a type
L(\Delta)\vdash A\ \mathsf{type}
—which gives rise to a type
R(L(\Delta))\vdash\textsf{R}(A)\ \mathsf{type}
by applying
R
—and a term
\Delta\vdash M:\textsf{R}(A)[\eta_{\Delta}]
. The universal property of the pullback dictates that we must
show the existence of a unique term
L(\Delta)\vdash N:A
such that

RL(\Delta)\vdash\textsf{r}(N)[\eta_{\Delta}]=M:\textsf{R}(A)[\eta_{\Delta}]

(16)

First, observe that we can form the substitution
{\eta_{\Delta}}.{M}:\Delta\to RL\Delta.\textsf{R}(A)
. We can then postcompose the isomorphism
\nu_{\Delta,A}
to
obtain a morphism of type
\Delta\to R(L\Delta.A)
. To this we can apply
L
and
postcompose the counit
\epsilon_{L\Delta.A}
to obtain a substitution

k\triangleq\epsilon_{L\Delta.A}\circ L(\nu_{\Delta,A}\circ{\eta_{\Delta}}.{M}):L\Delta\to L\Delta.A

Using naturality of the counit and the equations satisfied by the canonical isomorphism

\nu_{\Delta,A}
, it is easy to show that
\mathbf{p}\circ k=\mathsf{id}:L\Delta\to L\Delta
, and hence
that we can extract a term

L(\Delta)\vdash N\triangleq\mathbf{q}[k]:A

Using naturality of
\textsf{r}(-)
, naturality of the unit, and one of the triangle identities,
we can calculate that this term satisfies equation
(
16
). Finally, we can prove this choice is
unique by calculating that any such
N
necessarily satisfies
k=\mathsf{id}.N
, and hence that

\mathbf{q}[k]=N
.

It is routine to show that this is size-preserving, using the fact that
R
preserves
size.

The converse is not in general true: a dependent right adjoint need not extend to a functor on the
category of contexts. Nevertheless, it does whenever the category of contexts is
democratic

[
CD14
]
, i.e. if every context is isomorphic to extending the empty context by
some type: see
[
BCM+20
, §4.1]
for a proof.

8.
Presheaf Models

It is well-known that the category
\mathbf{PSh}(C)
of presheaves over any small category
\mathcal{C}
is a model
of Martin-Löf type theory. A functor
\mu:\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{D}
induces by
precomposition
a
functor

\mu^{*}:\mathbf{PSh}(\mathcal{D})\to\mathbf{PSh}(\mathcal{C})

between categories of presheaves. This functor has a right adjoint

\mu_{{*}}:\mathbf{PSh}(\mathcal{C})\to\mathbf{PSh}(\mathcal{D})

given by
right Kan extension

[
ML78
, §X.3]

[
Awo10
, §9.6]

[
Rie16
, §6]
. We show that an appropriately functorial version of this structure can be
bootstrapped into a model of
MTT
, where the modalities are right adjoints to this precomposition
functor. More concretely, starting with a small 2-category
\mathcal{I}
, and a functor

J:\mathcal{I}\to\mathbf{Cat}

we will construct a model of
MTT
where each mode corresponds to the category
\mathbf{PSh}(J(i))
, and the modalities are the functors
J(f)_{{*}}
, for each
f\in\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{I}}(i_{0},i_{1})
.

Context structure.

We define a strict 2-functor
\llbracket-\rrbracket:{\mathcal{I}}^{\mathsf{coop}}\to\mathbf{Cat}
by

\displaystyle i\

\displaystyle\longmapsto\mathcal{C}[i]\triangleq\mathbf{PSh}(J(i))

\displaystyle f:i\to j\
