\displaystyle\longmapsto\llbracket\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{f}\rrbracket\triangleq J(f)^{*}:\mathbf{PSh}(J(j))\to\mathbf{PSh}(J(i))

\displaystyle\alpha:f\Rightarrow g\

\displaystyle\longmapsto\llbracket\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.85}{$\displaystyle\text{\faIconFromMacro{faKey}}$}}{\scalebox{0.85}{$\textstyle\text{\faIconFromMacro{faKey}}$}}{\scalebox{0.85}{$\scriptstyle\text{\faIconFromMacro{faKey}}$}}{\scalebox{0.85}{$\scriptscriptstyle\text{\faIconFromMacro{faKey}}$}}^{\alpha}\rrbracket\triangleq J(\alpha)^{*}:J(g)^{*}\Rightarrow J(f)^{*}

The variance is correct: recall that precomposition is a strict 2-functor

(-)^{*}:{\mathbf{Cat}}^{\mathsf{coop}}\to\mathbf{Cat}

which maps a functor
f:\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{D}
to
f^{*}:\mathbf{PSh}(\mathcal{D})\to\mathbf{PSh}(\mathcal{C})
, and a natural
transformation
\alpha:f\Rightarrow g
to
\alpha^{*}:g^{*}\Rightarrow f^{*}
, given by

\alpha^{*}_{P,c}\triangleq P(\alpha_{c}):P(g(c))\to P(f(c))
. 2-functoriality is immediate, as for
example
J(f)^{*}\circ J(g)^{*}=(J(g)\circ J(f))^{*}=J(g\circ f)^{*}
.

Modal natural models.

To interpret the ‘mode-local’ structure we must construct a modal natural model in each

\mathcal{C}[i]
. It is well-known that every presheaf topos
\mathbf{PSh}(\mathcal{C})
gives rise to a rich model
of
MLTT
: see e.g.
[
Hof97
, §4.1]
or
[
Coq13
]
.

Contexts
are interpreted as objects of the presheaf category
\mathbf{PSh}(\mathcal{C})
.
Types
are
presheaves
\mathbf{PSh}(\textstyle\int\Gamma)
over the category of elements
\textstyle\int\Gamma
of a context
\Gamma:\mathbf{PSh}(\mathcal{C})
.
8
8

8

There is an equivalence
\mathbf{PSh}(\textstyle\int\Gamma)\simeq\mathbf{PSh}(\mathcal{C})/\Gamma

which shows that types are families
P\Rightarrow\Gamma
in the slice category. However, using the latter
definition would lead to strictness issues.
We define the action of a substitution
\sigma:\Delta\Rightarrow\Gamma
on a type
A:\mathbf{PSh}(\textstyle\int\Gamma)
by

A[\sigma]\triangleq{(\textstyle\int\Delta)}^{\mathsf{op}}\xrightarrow{{(\textstyle\int\sigma)}^{\mathsf{op}}}{(\textstyle\int\Gamma)}^{\mathsf{op}}\xrightarrow{A}\mathbf{Set}

This is functorial because
\textstyle\int-:\mathbf{PSh}(\mathcal{C})\to\mathbf{Cat}
and
{-}^{\mathsf{op}}:\mathbf{Cat}\to\mathbf{Cat}
are.

A
term
of type
A
is a global section of
A
, i.e. a morphism

\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbf{PSh}(\textstyle\int\Gamma)}(1,A)
. We define the action of a substitution
\sigma:\Delta\Rightarrow\Gamma

on a term
M:\mathrm{Hom}(1,A)
by whiskering:

M[\sigma]\triangleq M\ast{(\textstyle\int\sigma)}^{\mathsf{op}}:1\circ{(\textstyle\int\sigma)}^{\mathsf{op}}\Rightarrow A\circ{(\textstyle\int\sigma)}^{\mathsf{op}}=A[\sigma]

As
1\circ{\textstyle\int\sigma}^{\mathsf{op}}=1
, this has the right type. It is functorial because whiskering is.

Remark 11

(Size Issues)
.

One cannot be too careful with size issues when considering presheaf models. In
Section
5.1.2
we demanded that the category of contexts be
small
,
so that we can then formulate a large category of models.
\mathbf{PSh}(\mathcal{C})
is certainly not small. We
can mend this by assuming a Grothendieck universe
\mathcal{V}
large enough to contain
\mathcal{C}
in the
ambient set theory, and re-defining
\mathbf{PSh}(\mathcal{C})
to consist of the presheaves

P:{\mathcal{C}}^{\mathsf{op}}\to\mathcal{V}
with
small fibers
. As
\mathcal{V}
is closed under all set-theoretic
operations, this is still a model, and
\mathbf{PSh}(\mathcal{C})
is small.

To interpret universes we need to know that the fibers of types in
\mathbf{PSh}(\textstyle\int\mathcal{C})
are even

smaller
. Thus, we further assume a second, inner Grothendieck universe
\mathcal{V}^{\prime}\subset\mathcal{V}
.
To a type theorist, this is just the standard technique of ‘bumping’ a universe level.

Connectives.

Presheaf models support dependent sums and products, and extensional identity types (and therefore
intensional identity types): see
[
Hof97
, §4.2]
. On the premise that the underlying set
theory has a set-theoretic universe, they also support a universe, through a construction of

[
HS97
]
. See also
[
Coq13
]
.

Modalities.

It remains to show that each
\llbracket\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{f}\rrbracket\triangleq J(f)^{*}:\mathbf{PSh}(J(j))\to\mathbf{PSh}(J(i))
has a
corresponding modality acting on terms and types. We will do so by using previous results. By
Theorem
8
, it suffices to construct a
dependent right adjoint
to

J(f)^{*}
. But recall that each lock functor
J(f)^{*}
already has an (ordinary) right
adjoint, viz.
J(f)_{{*}}
. Thus, by Lemma
10
it will
suffice to show that the action of this right adjoint extends to types and terms. We then have DRAs,
and hence a model of
MTT
.

The following result has previously been shown in a tech report by the third author
[
Nuy18
, Prop.
2.2.9]
. We reproduce it here for the sake of completeness.

Lemma 12
.

The right adjoint to precomposition
\mu_{{*}}:\mathbf{PSh}(\mathcal{C})\to\mathbf{PSh}(\mathcal{D})
induces a DRA for any

\mu:\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{D}
. Moreover,
\mu_{{*}}
is size-preserving for any Grothendieck universe.

Proof 8.1
.

We use Lemma
10
once more. The action of
\mu_{{*}}
on types and terms is given by

\displaystyle\mu_{{*}}A\in\mathbf{PSh}(\textstyle\int\mu_{{*}}\Gamma)

\displaystyle=(D\in\mathcal{D},a\in\mu_{{*}}\Gamma(D))\mapsto\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbf{PSh}(\textstyle\int\mu^{*}(\mathbf{y}(D)))}(1,A\left[\widehat{\mathchoice{\left\lfloor\mkern-2.0mu{a}\mkern-2.0mu\right\rfloor}{\left\lfloor\mkern-2.0mu{a}\mkern-2.0mu\right\rfloor}{\lfloor a\rfloor}{\lfloor a\rfloor}}\right])

\displaystyle\mu_{{*}}M\in\mathrm{Hom}(1,\mu_{{*}}A)

\displaystyle=(D\in\mathcal{D},a\in\mu_{{*}}\Gamma(D))\mapsto(\textstyle\int\widehat{\mathchoice{\left\lfloor\mkern-2.0mu{a}\mkern-2.0mu\right\rfloor}{\left\lfloor\mkern-2.0mu{a}\mkern-2.0mu\right\rfloor}{\lfloor a\rfloor}{\lfloor a\rfloor}})^{*}M

Both of these actions are well-typed. For types, as
a\in\mu_{{*}}\Gamma(D)
we have

\mathchoice{\left\lfloor\mkern-2.0mu{a}\mkern-2.0mu\right\rfloor}{\left\lfloor\mkern-2.0mu{a}\mkern-2.0mu\right\rfloor}{\lfloor a\rfloor}{\lfloor a\rfloor}:\mathbf{y}(D)\Rightarrow\mu_{{*}}\Gamma
, so by transposition
\widehat{\mathchoice{\left\lfloor\mkern-2.0mu{a}\mkern-2.0mu\right\rfloor}{\left\lfloor\mkern-2.0mu{a}\mkern-2.0mu\right\rfloor}{\lfloor a\rfloor}{\lfloor a\rfloor}}:\mu^{*}(\mathbf{y}(D))\Rightarrow\Gamma
. For terms, notice that
\textstyle\int\widehat{\mathchoice{\left\lfloor\mkern-2.0mu{a}\mkern-2.0mu\right\rfloor}{\left\lfloor\mkern-2.0mu{a}\mkern-2.0mu\right\rfloor}{\lfloor a\rfloor}{\lfloor a\rfloor}}:\textstyle\int\mu^{*}(\mathbf{y}(D))\to\textstyle\int\Gamma
, recall that
A\left[\sigma\right]\triangleq A\circ\textstyle\int\sigma
, and that precomposition preserves the terminal object on-the-nose.

The presheaf action.

The action of
\mu_{{*}}A
is subtle: it is given by the functor

(\textstyle\int\mu^{*}\mathbf{y}(f))^{*}:\mathbf{PSh}(\textstyle\int\mu^{*}\mathbf{y}(D))\to\mathbf{PSh}((\textstyle\int\mu^{*}\mathbf{y}(D^{\prime}))

for each
f:\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{D}}(D^{\prime},D)
. In more detail, given
f:\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{D}}(D^{\prime},D)
,
a\in\mu_{{*}}\Gamma(D)
,
A\in\mathbf{PSh}(\textstyle\int\Gamma)
, and
x\in\mu_{{*}}A(D,a)\triangleq\mathrm{Hom}(1,A\left[\widehat{\mathchoice{\left\lfloor\mkern-2.0mu{a}\mkern-2.0mu\right\rfloor}{\left\lfloor\mkern-2.0mu{a}\mkern-2.0mu\right\rfloor}{\lfloor a\rfloor}{\lfloor a\rfloor}}\right])
, we define
x\cdot f\in\mu_{{*}}A(D^{\prime},a\cdot f)
by

x\cdot f\triangleq(\textstyle\int\mu^{*}\mathbf{y}(f))^{*}(x):\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbf{PSh}(\textstyle\int\mu^{*}(\mathbf{y}(D^{\prime})))}((\textstyle\int\mu^{*}\mathbf{y}(f))^{*}1,(\textstyle\int\mu^{*}\mathbf{y}(f))^{*}(A\left[\widehat{\mathchoice{\left\lfloor\mkern-2.0mu{a}\mkern-2.0mu\right\rfloor}{\left\lfloor\mkern-2.0mu{a}\mkern-2.0mu\right\rfloor}{\lfloor a\rfloor}{\lfloor a\rfloor}}\right]))

This is of the right type; reindexing preserves the terminal,

(\textstyle\int\mu^{*}\mathbf{y}(f))^{*}1=1
. Moreover,

\widehat{\mathchoice{\left\lfloor\mkern-2.0mu{a}\mkern-2.0mu\right\rfloor}{\left\lfloor\mkern-2.0mu{a}\mkern-2.0mu\right\rfloor}{\lfloor a\rfloor}{\lfloor a\rfloor}}\circ\mu^{*}\mathbf{y}(f)=\widehat{\mathchoice{\left\lfloor\mkern-2.0mu{a}\mkern-2.0mu\right\rfloor}{\left\lfloor\mkern-2.0mu{a}\mkern-2.0mu\right\rfloor}{\lfloor a\rfloor}{\lfloor a\rfloor}\circ\mathbf{y}(f)}=\widehat{\mathchoice{\left\lfloor\mkern-2.0mu{a\cdot f}\mkern-2.0mu\right\rfloor}{\left\lfloor\mkern-2.0mu{a\cdot f}\mkern-2.0mu\right\rfloor}{\lfloor a\cdot f\rfloor}{\lfloor a\cdot f\rfloor}}

by naturality of the adjunction and of Yoneda. Using this calculation, we see that

(\textstyle\int\mu^{*}\mathbf{y}(f))^{*}(A\left[\widehat{\mathchoice{\left\lfloor\mkern-2.0mu{a}\mkern-2.0mu\right\rfloor}{\left\lfloor\mkern-2.0mu{a}\mkern-2.0mu\right\rfloor}{\lfloor a\rfloor}{\lfloor a\rfloor}}\right])\triangleq A\circ\textstyle\int\widehat{\mathchoice{\left\lfloor\mkern-2.0mu{a}\mkern-2.0mu\right\rfloor}{\left\lfloor\mkern-2.0mu{a}\mkern-2.0mu\right\rfloor}{\lfloor a\rfloor}{\lfloor a\rfloor}}\circ\textstyle\int\mu^{*}\mathbf{y}(f)=A\circ\textstyle\int\widehat{\mathchoice{\left\lfloor\mkern-2.0mu{a\cdot f}\mkern-2.0mu\right\rfloor}{\left\lfloor\mkern-2.0mu{a\cdot f}\mkern-2.0mu\right\rfloor}{\lfloor a\cdot f\rfloor}{\lfloor a\cdot f\rfloor}}\triangleq A\left[\widehat{\mathchoice{\left\lfloor\mkern-2.0mu{a\cdot f}\mkern-2.0mu\right\rfloor}{\left\lfloor\mkern-2.0mu{a\cdot f}\mkern-2.0mu\right\rfloor}{\lfloor a\cdot f\rfloor}{\lfloor a\cdot f\rfloor}}\right]

Hence
x\cdot f\in\mu_{{*}}A(D^{\prime},a\cdot f)
. This assignment is functorial because

\textstyle\int-
,
(-)^{*}
and
\mathbf{y}(-)
are.

Naturality.

We must show that both of these definitions are natural with respect to substitution, i.e. that

(\mu_{{*}}A)\left[\mu_{{*}}\gamma\right]=\mu_{{*}}(A\left[\gamma\right])
, and similarly
for terms.

For types, suppose we are given
\gamma:\Delta\to\Gamma
and
A\in\mathbf{PSh}(\textstyle\int\Gamma)
.
Carefully unfolding both sides of the desired equation, for any
D\in\mathcal{D}
and
a\in\mu_{{*}}\Delta(D)
we must show that

\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbf{PSh}(\textstyle\int\mu^{*}\mathbf{y}(D))}(1,A\left[\widehat{\mathchoice{\left\lfloor\mkern-2.0mu{\mu_{{*}}\gamma_{D}(a)}\mkern-2.0mu\right\rfloor}{\left\lfloor\mkern-2.0mu{\mu_{{*}}\gamma_{D}(a)}\mkern-2.0mu\right\rfloor}{\lfloor\mu_{{*}}\gamma_{D}(a)\rfloor}{\lfloor\mu_{{*}}\gamma_{D}(a)\rfloor}}\right])=\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbf{PSh}(\textstyle\int\mu^{*}\mathbf{y}(D))}(1,A[\gamma]\left[\widehat{\mathchoice{\left\lfloor\mkern-2.0mu{a}\mkern-2.0mu\right\rfloor}{\left\lfloor\mkern-2.0mu{a}\mkern-2.0mu\right\rfloor}{\lfloor a\rfloor}{\lfloor a\rfloor}}\right])

But, by naturality of both the adjunction and Yoneda:

\gamma\circ\widehat{\mathchoice{\left\lfloor\mkern-2.0mu{a}\mkern-2.0mu\right\rfloor}{\left\lfloor\mkern-2.0mu{a}\mkern-2.0mu\right\rfloor}{\lfloor a\rfloor}{\lfloor a\rfloor}}=\widehat{\mu_{{*}}\gamma\circ\mathchoice{\left\lfloor\mkern-2.0mu{a}\mkern-2.0mu\right\rfloor}{\left\lfloor\mkern-2.0mu{a}\mkern-2.0mu\right\rfloor}{\lfloor a\rfloor}{\lfloor a\rfloor}}=\widehat{\mathchoice{\left\lfloor\mkern-2.0mu{\mu_{{*}}\gamma_{D}(a)}\mkern-2.0mu\right\rfloor}{\left\lfloor\mkern-2.0mu{\mu_{{*}}\gamma_{D}(a)}\mkern-2.0mu\right\rfloor}{\lfloor\mu_{{*}}\gamma_{D}(a)\rfloor}{\lfloor\mu_{{*}}\gamma_{D}(a)\rfloor}}

Hence the two sets are the same. The calculation for terms is of a similar ilk.

Preservation of context extension.

We would like to show that the canonical morphism

\mu_{{*}}(\Gamma.A)

\mu_{{*}}\Gamma.\mu_{{*}}A
\scriptstyle\langle\mu_{{*}}\mathbf{p},\mu_{{*}}\mathbf{q}\rangle

is invertible. Consider an element
e:\mathbf{y}(D)\Rightarrow\mu_{{*}}(\Gamma.A)
. We can
transpose along the adjunction
\mu^{*}\dashv\mu_{{*}}
and decompose it to obtain a
substitution and a term

\displaystyle e_{0}

\displaystyle:\mu^{*}\mathbf{y}(D)\Rightarrow\Gamma

\displaystyle e_{1}

\displaystyle:\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbf{PSh}(\textstyle\int\mu^{*}\mathbf{y}(D))}(1,A\left[e_{0}\right])

We can thus write
e=\widehat{\langle e_{0},e_{1}\rangle}
. Thus, we can use naturality of the
adjunction and of substitution to compute the action of
\langle\mu_{{*}}\mathbf{p},\mu_{{*}}\mathbf{q}\rangle
on this
e
:

\langle\mu_{{*}}\mathbf{p},\mu_{{*}}\mathbf{q}\rangle\circ e=\langle\mu_{{*}}\mathbf{p}\circ e,(\mu_{{*}}\mathbf{q})\left[e\right]\rangle\\
=\langle\widehat{\mathbf{p}\circ\langle e_{0},e_{1}\rangle},\mathbf{q}[\langle e_{0},e_{1}\rangle]\rangle\\
=\langle\widehat{e}_{0},e_{1}\rangle

We can then specify an inverse on generalized elements by
\langle\gamma,M\rangle\mapsto\widehat{\langle\widehat{\gamma},M\rangle}
.

Size preservation is immediate: if
A
is small then so are its reindexings and the collections of
points at each slice.

8.1.
The other adjunction

We have so far concentrated on the adjunction
\mu^{*}\dashv\mu_{{*}}
that arises through right Kan extension. Nevertheless, precomposition also has a left adjoint
\mu_{{!}}
arising from
left Kan extension
. Might we also be able to interpret the lock functors by this left adjoint
\mu_{{!}}
, and lift precomposition
\mu^{*}
to a modality instead?

It is in fact relatively easy to show that
\mu^{*}
extends to a dependent right adjoint. However, the
left Kan extensions
\mu_{{!}}
cannot be assembled into a modal context structure. The reason is
that context structures are strict 2-functors, but left Kan extensions do not compose strictly: we
only have an isomorphism
F_{{!}}\circ G_{{!}}\cong(G\circ F)_{{!}}
. We have proven a
strictification theorem that straightens these issues, but that is beyond the scope of this paper.

9.
Guarded Recursion

We now show how
MTT
can be applied to a well-known modal situation: guarded recursion. By
instantiating
MTT
with a carefully chosen mode theory and axiomitizing certain operations
specific to guarded recursion (i.e. Löb induction), we obtain a calculus for guarded recursion
simpler than prior hand-crafted calculi. We demonstrate the practicality of this guarded variant of

MTT
by reproducing some examples from prior work on guarded recursion
[
BGC+16
]
.

The key idea of guarded recursion
[
Nak00
]
is to use a modality
\RHD
, usually called

later
, to mark the types of data that may be used only if some ‘computational progress’ (e.g.
a tick of a clock) has taken place, thereby enforcing productivity at the level of types. The later
modality is usually equipped with three basic operations:

\mathsf{next}:A\to\RHD A(\mathbin{\circledast}):\RHD(A\to B)\to\RHD A\to\RHD B\textnormal{l{\"{o}}b}:(\RHD A\to A)\to A

The first two make
\RHD
into an
applicative functor

[
MP08
]
. The third,
which is commonly known as Löb induction, is a
guarded fixed point operator

[
ML13
]
: it enables us to make definitions by
provably productive
recursion.

\RHD
also applies to the universe, so one can define data types by guarded recursion. The
classic example is the
guarded stream type

\mathsf{Str}_{A}\cong A\times\RHD\mathsf{Str}_{A}
, with
constructor

\mathsf{cons}_{A}:A\times\RHD\mathsf{Str}_{A}\cong\mathsf{Str}_{A}

The presence of the modality enforces the requirement that the head of the stream is available
immediately, but the tail may only be accessed after some productive work has taken place. This
allows us to e.g. construct an infinite stream of ones:

\mathsf{inf\_stream\_of\_ones}\triangleq\textnormal{l{\"{o}}b}(s.\ \mathsf{cons}(1,s))

Unlike the ordinary type of streams,
\mathsf{Str}_{A}
does not behave like a coinductive type: we may only
define
causal
operations, which excludes useful functions (e.g. the
\mathsf{tail}
function). In
order to regain coinductive behaviour,
[
CBGB15
]
introduced the
always

modality
\Box
, an idempotent comonad for which

\Box\RHD A\simeq\Box A.

(
\ast
)

Combining
\Box
and
\RHD
in the same system has proven tricky. Previous work has used

delayed substitutions

[
BGC+16
]
, or replaced
\Box
with
clock
quantification

[
AM13
,
Møg14
,
BM15
,
BGM17
]
. Neither solution is
entirely satisfactory: the former poses serious implementation and usability issues, and the latter
does not enjoy the conceptual simplicity of a single modality. We will show that
MTT
enables us
to effortlessly combine the two modalities whilst satisfying (

∗

‣
9
).

To encode guarded recursion inside
MTT
, we must

(1)

construct a mode theory that induces an applicative functor
\RHD
and an idempotent comonad

\Box
satisfying (

∗

‣
9
),

(2)

construct the
intended model
of
MTT
with this mode theory, i.e. a model where
these modalities are interpreted in the standard way
[
BMSS12
]
, and

(3)

include Löb induction as an axiom, and use it to reason about guarded streams.

9.1.
A guarded mode theory

t
s
\ell
\delta
\gamma
\begin{aligned} \delta\circ\gamma&\leq 1&1&=\gamma\circ\delta\\
1&\leq\ell&\gamma&=\gamma\circ\ell\end{aligned}

Figure 11.
The ‘adjoint bowling pin’
\mathcal{M}_{g}
: a mode theory for guarded recursion.

We define
\mathcal{M}_{g}
to be the mode theory generated by the graph and equations of Fig.

11
. We require that
\mathcal{M}_{g}
be poset-enriched,
i.e. that there be at most one 2-cell between a pair of modalities
\mu,\nu
, which we denote by

\mu\leq\nu
when it exists. Consequently, we need not state any coherence equations between
2-cells.

Unlike prior guarded type theories,
\mathcal{M}_{g}
has
two modes
. We will think of elements
of
s
as being
constant types and terms
, while types in
t
may
vary over time
.
Observe that we can thence construct a composite modality
b\triangleq\delta\circ\gamma
. Moreover,
this modality is idempotent, for
b\circ b=\delta\circ\gamma\circ\delta\circ\gamma=\delta\circ\gamma=b
. We will prove in Section
9.3
that

Lemma 13
.

\langle b\mid-\rangle
is an idempotent comonad and
\langle\ell\mid-\rangle
is an applicative functor.

9.2.
Decomposing the standard model

The above mode theory arises from a careful and informative decomposition of the standard model of
guarded recursion, namely the
topos of trees

\mathbf{PSh}(\omega)
, along with the later and always endomodalities.

The topos of trees consists of presheaves over the natural numbers, seen as a poset with the usual
order. An element
x_{n}\in X(n)
of a presheaf
X:\mathbf{PSh}(\omega)
represents an element computed
after
n
steps of computation. The restriction maps
r_{n}:X(n+1)\to X(n)
trim an element
computed after
n+1
steps to its form at the preceding moment in time. The canonical example is
given by
X(n)\triangleq\{\text{streams of length $n$}\}
, where
r_{n}
deletes the last element of a
stream of length
n+1
. The later and always endomodalities are given by delaying the computation by
one step, and by taking global sections (total elements):

\displaystyle(\RHD X)(n)

\displaystyle\triangleq{\begin{cases}\{\ast\}&\text{if $n=0$}\\
X(n-1)&\text{if $n>0$}\end{cases}}

\displaystyle(\Box X)(n)

\displaystyle\triangleq\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbf{PSh}(\omega)}(1,X)

To arrive at the mode theory above, one must notice that the comonad
\Box
results in a

constant
presheaf, namely one which consists of the same set at each time. We can thus
decompose it into the adjunction

\mathbf{PSh}(\omega)
\mathbf{Set}
\dashv
\RHD
\Gamma
\Delta

(17)

\Gamma
maps
X:\mathbf{PSh}(\omega)
to the set of its global sections
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbf{PSh}(\omega)}(1,X)
, and

\Delta
maps a set
S
to the constant presheaf
(\Delta S)(n)\triangleq S
. It is well-known that

\Delta\dashv\Gamma
, and ‘always’ is given by the induced comonad
\Box\triangleq\Delta\circ\Gamma
. This explains the provenance of the two modes in Figure

11
:
s
stands for
sets
, and
t
for
timed
sets
, i.e. presheaves over
\omega
.

We want to bootstrap (
17
) into a model of
MTT
. We will do so by leveraging
an impressive sequence of facts:

•

Both categories in (
17
) are presheaf categories, and hence models of

MLTT
: see Section
8
.

•

Every functor in (
17
) is a right adjoint.

•

The corresponding left adjoints are introduced by precomposition, and hence can easily be
arranged into a modal context structure for the mode theory
\mathcal{M}_{g}
as per
Section
5.1.1
.

•

Hence, by uniqueness of adjoints the functors in (
17
) are induced by
right Kan extension. Consequently, they can be bootstrapped into dependent right adjoints, by
Lemma
12
.

•

Therefore, by Theorem
8
, this data yields a model of
MTT
with mode theory
\mathcal{M}_{g}
.

Let us elaborate on this chain of reasoning.
First, we identify the category
\mathbf{Set}
and the category
\mathbf{PSh}(1)
of presheaves over the terminal
category. Second, we construct the two left adjoints. As
\omega
has an initial object
0
, we
obtain a left adjoint to the discrete functor
\Delta
, given by

\Pi_{0}(X)\triangleq X(0)

It is easy to see that
\Pi_{0}\dashv\Delta
: by naturality at the unique morphism
0\leq n
we
see that any
\alpha:X\Rightarrow\Delta S
is fully determined by the component
\alpha_{0}:X(0)\to S
.
Furthermore, recall from the work of
[
BMSS12
]
that the later modality
\RHD
has a
left adjoint
\LHD:\mathbf{PSh}(\omega)\to\mathbf{PSh}(\omega)
(pronounced ‘earlier’), given by

(\LHD X)(n)\triangleq X(n+1)

It remains to show that the three left adjoints—
\Pi_{0}
,
\Delta
, and
\LHD
—are given by
precomposition. We define three monotone functions between the posets
1\triangleq\{\ast\}
and

\omega
:

\displaystyle K_{0}:\

\displaystyle 1\

\displaystyle\to\

\displaystyle\omega\ \qquad

\displaystyle{!}_{\omega}\ :\

\displaystyle\omega\

\displaystyle\to\

\displaystyle 1\ \qquad

\displaystyle l\ :\

\displaystyle\omega\

\displaystyle\to\

\displaystyle\omega

\displaystyle\ast\

\displaystyle\mapsto\

\displaystyle 0\ \qquad

\displaystyle n\

\displaystyle\mapsto\

\displaystyle\ast\ \qquad

\displaystyle n\

\displaystyle\mapsto\

\displaystyle n+1

Identifying
\mathbf{Set}
with
\mathbf{PSh}(1)
, we see that

\displaystyle\Pi_{0}

\displaystyle=K_{0}^{*}:\mathbf{PSh}(\omega)\to\mathbf{Set}

\displaystyle\Delta

\displaystyle={!}_{\omega}^{*}:\mathbf{Set}\to\mathbf{PSh}(\omega)

\displaystyle\LHD

\displaystyle=l^{*}:\mathbf{PSh}(\omega)\to\mathbf{PSh}(\omega)

Moreover, we trivially have the following pointwise equations and inequalities:

\displaystyle\text{id}_{\omega}

\displaystyle\leq l

\displaystyle K_{0}\circ{!}_{\omega}

\displaystyle\leq\text{id}_{\omega}

\displaystyle\text{id}_{1}

\displaystyle={!}_{\omega}\circ K_{0}

\displaystyle{!}_{\omega}

\displaystyle={!}_{\omega}\circ l

Seeing posets as categories, pointwise inequalities are simply natural transformations between
monotone maps. By feeding them into the strict 2-functor
(-)^{*}:{\mathbf{Cat}}^{\mathsf{coop}}\to\mathbf{Cat}
, we
are able to define a strict 2-functor
\llbracket\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{-}\rrbracket:{\mathcal{M}_{g}}^{\mathsf{coop}}\to\mathbf{Cat}
which maps

\displaystyle\gamma\

\displaystyle:t\to s\quad

\displaystyle\longmapsto\quad

\displaystyle\llbracket\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{\gamma}\rrbracket=\Delta\

\displaystyle:\ \mathbf{Set}\to\mathbf{PSh}(\omega)

\displaystyle\delta\

\displaystyle:s\to t\quad

\displaystyle\longmapsto\quad

\displaystyle\llbracket\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{\delta}\rrbracket=\Pi_{0}\

\displaystyle:\ \mathbf{PSh}(\omega)\to\mathbf{Set}

\displaystyle\ell\

\displaystyle:t\to t\quad

\displaystyle\longmapsto\quad

\displaystyle\llbracket\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{\ell}\rrbracket=\LHD\

\displaystyle:\ \mathbf{PSh}(\omega)\to\mathbf{PSh}(\omega)

This fully specifies the modal context structure, which consists of left adjoints. Each of these
left adjoints is given by precomposition. Thus, the unique corresponding right adjoint is given by
right Kan extension (see Section
8
). Hence, by Lemma

12
and Theorem
8
,

Theorem 14
.

There is a model of
MTT
with mode theory
\mathcal{M}_{g}
, interpreting
s
as
\mathbf{Set}
and
t
as

\mathbf{PSh}(\omega)
. Furthermore, this model interprets
\delta
by the dependent right adjoint arising
from
\Pi_{0}\dashv\Delta
,
\gamma
by
\Delta\dashv\Gamma
, and
\ell
by
\LHD\dashv\RHD
.

Remark 15
.

This mode theory is a poset-enriched category. As a result, the key substitutions are unique: for
any
\mu,\nu
there is at most one substitution
\Gamma.\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{\mu}\vdash\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.85}{$\displaystyle\text{\faIconFromMacro{faKey}}$}}{\scalebox{0.85}{$\textstyle\text{\faIconFromMacro{faKey}}$}}{\scalebox{0.85}{$\scriptstyle\text{\faIconFromMacro{faKey}}$}}{\scalebox{0.85}{$\scriptscriptstyle\text{\faIconFromMacro{faKey}}$}}_{\Gamma}^{\nu\leq\mu}:\Gamma.\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{\nu}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{}\mathop{@}m}
. This property means that we can elide them without
ambiguity. However, this may sometimes make type-checking on pen-and-paper difficult, so we employ
a simplified notation: we will write
A^{\nu\leq\mu}
or
M^{\nu\leq\mu}
for the application of
the unique key substitution
\nu\leq\mu
in context
\Gamma.\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{\mu}
. For instance, given a type

\Gamma.\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{1}=\Gamma\vdash A\ \mathsf{type}_{1}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{}\mathop{@}{t}}
we can form the type
\Gamma.\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{\ell}\vdash A^{1\leq\ell}\ \mathsf{type}_{1}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{}\mathop{@}{t}}
, and hence the type
\Gamma\vdash\langle\ell\mid A^{1\leq\ell}\rangle\ \mathsf{type}_{1}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{}\mathop{@}{t}}
.

9.3.
Guarded recursion, internally

Given the model that we constructed above, we feel perfectly justified in defining the following
shorthands within
MTT
:

\displaystyle\Box A

\displaystyle\triangleq\langle b\mid A\rangle

\displaystyle\RHD A

\displaystyle\triangleq\langle\ell\mid A\rangle

\displaystyle\Gamma A

\displaystyle\triangleq\langle\gamma\mid A\rangle

\displaystyle\Delta A

\displaystyle\triangleq\langle\delta\mid A\rangle

where
b\triangleq\delta\circ\gamma
. The aim of this section is to show that
MTT
equipped with
\mathcal{M}_{g}
and these shorthands can be used to reason about guarded recursion. In particular, we will show that this is strict improvement on previous solutions, by establishing that

(1)

When restricted to mode
s
, the type theory is simply standard Martin-Löf Type Theory.

(2)

The modalities on mode
t
give rise to the standard modalities and operations of
Guarded Type Theory
[
BGC+16
]
inside the type theory.

First, we wish to show that if we restrict ourselves to endomodalities
\mu\in\mathrm{Hom}(s,s)
from
sets to sets, the type theory is just
MLTT
. Looking at Fig.

11
as a finite state machine, we see that all loops on
s

are of the form
\gamma\circ\ell^{n}\circ\delta
, and the equations of
\mathcal{M}_{g}
allow us to
prove that they are all equal to the identity
1_{s}
. It follows that
\langle\mu\mid A\rangle\simeq A
. Finally,
as there is no non-trivial 2-cell
1_{s}\Rightarrow 1_{s}
the variable rule reduces to

\frac{\mu\in\mathrm{Hom}(s,s)\quad\Gamma\ \mathsf{ctx}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{}\mathop{@}s}\quad\Gamma\vdash A\ \mathsf{type}_{\ell}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{}\mathop{@}{s}}\quad(x:(\mu\mid A))\in\Gamma}{\Gamma\vdash x:A{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{}\mathop{@}s}}

which is essentially the usual variable rule of
MLTT
.

Second, we use the combinators of Section
3.2
to prove that
\Box
is an
idempotent comonad.

\begin{array}[]{lcllcl}\mathsf{dup}_{A}&:&\Box A\xrightarrow{\simeq}\Box\Box A&\mathsf{extract}_{A}&:&\Box A\to A^{b\leq 1}\\
\mathsf{dup}_{A}(x)&\triangleq&\mathbf{comp}_{b,b}^{\text{-1}}(x)&\mathsf{extract}_{A}(x)&\triangleq&\mathbf{triv}^{\text{-1}}(\mathbf{coe}[b\leq 1](x))\end{array}

Recall the
K
operator
-\mathbin{\circledast_{b}}-:\Box(A\to B)\to\Box A\to\Box B
for the modality
b
,
which was defined in Section
3.1
. Writing
\mathsf{box}(M)\triangleq\mathsf{mod}_{b}(M)
, the claim that
\Box
is an internal idempotent comonad amounts to defining terms of
the following types.

\displaystyle(x:\Box A)

\displaystyle\to\mathsf{Id}_{\Box A}(x,\mathsf{box}(\mathsf{extract})\mathbin{\circledast_{b}}\mathsf{dup}(x))

(18)

\displaystyle(x:\Box A)

\displaystyle\to\mathsf{Id}_{\Box A}(x,\mathsf{extract}(\mathsf{dup}(x)))

(19)

\displaystyle(x:\Box A)

\displaystyle\to\mathsf{Id}_{\Box\Box\Box A}(\mathsf{dup}(\mathsf{dup}(x)),\mathsf{box}(\mathsf{dup})\mathbin{\circledast_{b}}\mathsf{dup}(x))

(20)

These can be constructed by unfolding and modal induction on
x:\Box A
.

The
K
operator
-\mathbin{\circledast_{\ell}}-:\RHD(A\to B)\to\RHD A\to\RHD B
for the modality

\ell
almost proves that
\RHD
is an applicative functor. It remains to show that
\RHD
is
pointed:

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\mathsf{next}_{A}&:&A\to\RHD A\\
\mathsf{next}_{A}(x)&\triangleq&\mathbf{coe}[1\leq\ell](\mathbf{triv}(x))\end{array}

Next, we show the defining equivalence (

∗

‣
9
). We calculate that
b\circ\ell\triangleq\delta\circ\gamma\circ\ell=\delta\circ\gamma\triangleq b
, and hence that the equivalence is a
corollary of a combinator given in Section
3.1
:

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\mathsf{now}_{A}(x)&:&\Box\RHD A\xrightarrow{\simeq}\Box A\\
\mathsf{now}_{A}(x)&\triangleq&\mathbf{comp}_{b,\ell}^{\text{-1}}(x)\end{array}

As a sanity check, we can compute that the following composite is the identity:

\Box A
\Box\RHD A
\Box A
\mathsf{box}(\mathsf{next})\mathbin{\circledast}-
\mathsf{now}

The calculation is as follows:

\displaystyle\mathbf{comp}_{b,\ell}(\mathsf{mod}_{b}(\mathbf{coe}[1\leq\ell](\mathbf{triv}(-)))\mathbin{\circledast}x)

by induction, suppose
x=\mathsf{mod}_{b}(y)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{comp}_{b,\ell}(\mathsf{mod}_{b}(\mathbf{coe}[1\leq\ell](\mathbf{triv}(-)))\mathbin{\circledast}\mathsf{mod}_{b}(y))

\displaystyle=\mathbf{comp}_{b,\ell}(\mathsf{mod}_{b}(\mathbf{coe}[1\leq\ell](\mathbf{triv}(y))))

\displaystyle=\mathbf{comp}_{b,\ell}(\mathsf{mod}_{b}(\mathsf{mod}_{\ell}(y)))

\displaystyle=\mathsf{mod}_{b}(y)

as
b\circ\ell=b

\displaystyle=x

The only thing that remains is to add
Löb induction
. This is a
modality-specific
operation
that cannot be expressed in the mode theory, so we must add it as an axiom:

\frac{\Gamma\ \mathsf{ctx}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{}\mathop{@}t}\quad\Gamma\vdash A\ \mathsf{type}_{1}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{}\mathop{@}{t}}}{\Gamma\vdash\textnormal{l{\"{o}}b}:(\RHD A^{1\leq\ell}\to A)\to A{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{}\mathop{@}t}}\frac{\Gamma\ \mathsf{ctx}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{}\mathop{@}t}\quad\Gamma\vdash A\ \mathsf{type}_{1}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{}\mathop{@}{t}}\quad\Gamma\vdash M:\RHD A^{1\leq\ell}\to A{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{}\mathop{@}t}}{\Gamma\vdash\textnormal{l{\"{o}}b}(M)=M(\mathsf{next}(\textnormal{l{\"{o}}b}(M))):(\RHD A^{1\leq\ell}\to A)\to A{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{}\mathop{@}t}}

Notice that these rules are only added in mode
t
, as they only admit an interpretation in

\mathbf{PSh}(\omega)

[
BMSS12
, §2]
. Unfortunately, these ad-hoc additions mean that the
canonicity theorem of Section
6
no longer applies.

9.4.
Reasoning about Streams

We now put
MTT
to work: we will use it to reason about infinite streams defined by guarded
recursion. We will demonstrate that the rules and axioms given in
Section
9.3
suffice to carry out coinductive constructions. In
particular, we will reproduce an example of
[
BGC+16
]
: we will show that
\mathsf{zipWith}(f)
on
a coinductive stream is commutative whenever
f
itself is.

In order to simplify our working, we will swap the intensional equality type
\mathsf{Id}_{A}(M,N)
with an

extensional identity type

\mathsf{Eq}_{A}(M,N)
. This has the same introduction rule, but its
elimination is replaced by the usual
equality reflection rule

\frac{\Gamma\vdash P:\mathsf{Eq}_{A}(M_{0},M_{1}){\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{}\mathop{@}m}}{\Gamma\vdash M_{0}=M_{1}:A{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{}\mathop{@}m}}

This is straightforwardly interpreted in the model, as both modes are mapped to presheaf toposes.
The switch to extensional equality is not strictly necessary: we could carry out the following
calculations with intensional identity, at the price of significantly more verbose terms. Moreover,
the need for the function extensionality axiom would arise. However, adding Löb induction has
already ensured that type-checking is undecidable, so nothing of value is lost by making the switch
to extensional type theory for these examples.

We begin with a simple reasoning principle. Eliding
(-)^{1\leq\ell}
annotations:

Lemma 16
.

(A:\mathsf{U})(x,y:\mathsf{El}(A))\to\RHD\mathsf{Eq}_{\mathsf{El}(A)}(x,y)\to\mathsf{Eq}_{\RHD\mathsf{El}(A)}(\mathsf{next}(x),\mathsf{next}(y))

Proof 9.1
.

Suppose
x,y:\mathsf{El}(A)
and
p:\RHD\mathsf{Eq}_{\mathsf{El}(A)}(x,y)
; to show
\mathsf{next}(x)=\mathsf{next}(y):\RHD\mathsf{El}(A)
. By congruence and the elimination rule for the modality, it suffices to prove
x=y:\mathsf{El}(A)
in the locked context
A:\mathsf{U},x:\mathsf{El}(A),y:\mathsf{El}(A),p:(\ell\mid\mathsf{Eq}_{\mathsf{El}(A)}(x,y)),\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{\ell}
. But by the variable rule we have
p:\mathsf{Eq}_{\mathsf{El}(A)}(x,y)
in this context, and hence
x=y:\mathsf{El}(A)
.

This can be used to prove
internally
that guarded fixed points are unique.

Theorem 17
.

\textnormal{l{\"{o}}b}(M)
is the unique guarded fixed point of
M:\RHD\mathsf{El}(A)\to\mathsf{El}(A)
,
i.e.

(A:\mathsf{U})(x:\mathsf{El}(A))\to\mathsf{Eq}_{\mathsf{El}(A)}(M(\mathsf{next}(x)),x)\to\mathsf{Eq}_{\mathsf{El}(A)}(\textnormal{l{\"{o}}b}(M),x)

Proof 9.2
.

Suppose
A:\mathsf{U}
; to show
(x:\mathsf{El}(A))\to\mathsf{Eq}_{\mathsf{El}(A)}(M(\mathsf{next}(x)),x)\to\mathsf{Eq}_{\mathsf{El}(A)}(\textnormal{l{\"{o}}b}(M),x)
by Löb induction. Thus, assume that

f:\RHD((x:\mathsf{El}(A))\to\mathsf{Eq}_{\mathsf{El}(A)}(M(\mathsf{next}(x)),x)\to\mathsf{Eq}_{\mathsf{El}(A)}(\textnormal{l{\"{o}}b}(M),x))

If
x:A
and
p:\mathsf{Eq}_{\mathsf{El}(A)}(M(\mathsf{next}(x)),x)
, we calculate that

\displaystyle\textnormal{l{\"{o}}b}(M)

\displaystyle=M(\mathsf{next}(\textnormal{l{\"{o}}b}(M)))

unfolding rule for
löb

\displaystyle=M(\mathsf{next}(x))

by Lemma
16
on
f\mathbin{\circledast}\mathsf{next}(x)\mathbin{\circledast}\mathsf{next}(p):\RHD\mathsf{Eq}_{\mathsf{El}(A)}(\textnormal{l{\"{o}}b}(M),x)

\displaystyle=x

by
p

Thus, this type is inhabited by the term
\lambda A.\ \textnormal{l{\"{o}}b}(\lambda f.\ \lambda x.\ \lambda p.\ \mathsf{refl}(x))
.

We can also use the Löb operator on the universe to form
guarded recursive types
. For
example, streams can be defined by
9
9

9

We denote modalities and their counterparts on the
universe by the same notation. For example, we may write
\Delta A
to mean the type

\Gamma\vdash\langle\delta\mid A\rangle\ \mathsf{type}_{1}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{}\mathop{@}{m}}
whenever
\Gamma,\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{\delta}\vdash A\ \mathsf{type}_{1}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{}\mathop{@}{m}}
, but also to mean
the term
\Gamma\vdash\mathsf{Code}(\langle\delta\mid\mathsf{El}(A)\rangle):\mathsf{U}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{}\mathop{@}m}
whenever

\Gamma,\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{\delta}\vdash A:\mathsf{U}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{}\mathop{@}m}
.

\displaystyle\mathsf{Str}

\displaystyle:\

\displaystyle\mathsf{U}\to\mathsf{U}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{}\mathop{@}s}

\displaystyle\mathsf{Str}(A)

\displaystyle\triangleq\

\displaystyle\Gamma(\textnormal{l{\"{o}}b}(\lambda X.\ \Delta A\times\mathsf{let}\ \mathsf{mod}_{\ell}(Y)\leftarrow X\ \mathsf{in}\ \RHD Y))

\mathsf{Str}
maps a constant set, i.e. a type
A{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{}\mathop{@}s}
, to the type of streams over
A
,
which is again a constant set. This is done by first defining a timed set

A:(1\mid\mathsf{U}),\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{\gamma}\vdash\mathsf{Str}^{\prime}(A)\triangleq\textnormal{l{\"{o}}b}(\lambda X.\ \Delta A\times\mathsf{let}\ \mathsf{mod}_{\ell}(Y)\leftarrow X\ \mathsf{in}\ \RHD Y):\mathsf{U}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{}\mathop{@}t}

\mathsf{Str}^{\prime}(A)
is defined by Löb induction: assuming
X:(1\mid\RHD\mathsf{U}^{1\leq\ell})
we
must define an element of the timed universe. This is given as the product of

•

the set
A{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{}\mathop{@}s}
, considered as a constant-everywhere timed set
\Delta A{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{}\mathop{@}t}
;

•

a guarded recursive call, which represents the rest of the stream.

Recalling that
\mathsf{U}^{1\leq\ell}=\mathsf{U}
, the second component is given by modal elimination.
Nevertheless, it is not immediate that the first component type-checks: we must show that

A:(1\mid\mathsf{U}),\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{\gamma},\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{\delta}\vdash A:\mathsf{U}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{}\mathop{@}s}

But
\gamma\circ\delta=1
, so the context is equal to
A:(1\mid\mathsf{U}),\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{1}
and we
can use
A
. Unfolding the guarded fixed point, we have that

\mathsf{Str}^{\prime}(A)=\Delta{A}\times\RHD\mathsf{Str}^{\prime}(A):U{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{}\mathop{@}t}

We apply
\Gamma
to ‘totalize’ this into the constant set
\mathsf{Str}(A){\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{}\mathop{@}s}
of
guarded streams.

Even though not immediately obvious, there is a serious advantage in expressing this definition in a
way that spans two modes. In previous work
[
BGC+16
]
the stream type
\mathsf{Str}(A)
was
coinductive only if
A
was provably a ‘constant set,’ i.e. if
A\simeq\Box A
. Theorems about
streams had to carry around a proof of this equivalence. In our case, defining
\mathsf{Str}(A)
at the mode

s
of constant sets automatically ensures that. Hence,
\mathsf{Str}(A)
is equivalent to the familiar
definition, but we no longer need to propagate proofs of constancy.

\mathsf{Str}(A)
supports the following operations:

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\mathsf{cons}&:&(A:\mathsf{U})\to\mathsf{El}(A)\to\mathsf{El}(\mathsf{Str}(A))\to\mathsf{El}(\mathsf{Str}(A)){\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{}\mathop{@}s}\\
\mathsf{cons}_{A}(h,t)&\triangleq&\mathsf{let}\ \mathsf{mod}_{\gamma}(t^{\prime})\leftarrow t\ \mathsf{in}\ \mathsf{mod}_{\gamma}((\mathsf{mod}_{\delta}(h),\mathsf{next}(t^{\prime})))\\[8.5359pt]
\mathsf{head}&:&(A:\mathsf{U})\to\mathsf{El}(\mathsf{Str}(A))\to\mathsf{El}(A){\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{}\mathop{@}s}\\
\mathsf{head}_{A}(s)&\triangleq&\mathsf{let}\ \mathsf{mod}_{\gamma}(s^{\prime})\leftarrow s\ \mathsf{in}\ \mathbf{triv}^{\text{-1}}(\mathbf{comp}_{\gamma,\delta}(\mathsf{mod}_{\gamma}(\mathsf{pr}_{0}(s^{\prime}))))\\[8.5359pt]
\mathsf{tail}&:&(A:\mathsf{U})\to\mathsf{El}(\mathsf{Str}(A))\to\mathsf{El}(\mathsf{Str}(A)){\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{}\mathop{@}s}\\
\mathsf{tail}_{A}(s)&\triangleq&\mathsf{let}\ \mathsf{mod}_{\gamma}(s^{\prime})\leftarrow s\ \mathsf{in}\ \mathbf{comp}_{\gamma,\ell}(\mathsf{mod}_{\gamma}(\mathsf{pr}_{1}(s^{\prime})))\end{array}

Those familiar with prior work on guarded streams may be surprised by the type of
\mathsf{tail}
. The
expected definition would be

\mathsf{tail}_{A}(s)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle?}}{{=}}\mathsf{let}\ \mathsf{mod}_{\gamma}(s^{\prime})\leftarrow s\ \mathsf{in}\ \mathsf{mod}_{\gamma}(\mathsf{pr}_{1}(s^{\prime}))

This term has type
\mathsf{El}(\mathsf{Str}(A))\to\Gamma(\RHD\mathsf{El}(\mathsf{Str}^{\prime}(A)))
. However, in our case the

\Gamma
modality is sufficiently strong to “absorb” this extra
\RHD
: the equality
\gamma\circ\ell=\gamma
induces an equivalence
\Gamma\circ\RHD\simeq\Gamma
, which we use to
obtain the version given above. This small difference is crucial: it will internally make
\mathsf{Str}(A)

into a final coalgebra!

Lemma 18
.

These operations satisfy the expected
\beta
and
\eta
laws, i.e.

(1)

(h:\mathsf{El}(A))(t:\mathsf{El}(\mathsf{Str}(A)))\to\mathsf{Eq}_{\mathsf{El}(A)}(\mathsf{head}_{A}(\mathsf{cons}_{A}(h,t)),h){\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{}\mathop{@}s}

(2)

(h:\mathsf{El}(A))(t:\mathsf{El}(\mathsf{Str}(A)))\to\mathsf{Eq}_{\mathsf{El}(\mathsf{Str}(A))}(\mathsf{tail}_{A}(\mathsf{cons}_{A}(h,t)),t){\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{}\mathop{@}s}

(3)

(h:\mathsf{El}(A))(t:\mathsf{El}(\mathsf{Str}(A)))\to\mathsf{Eq}_{\mathsf{El}(\mathsf{Str}(A))}(s,\mathsf{cons}_{A}(\mathsf{head}_{A}(s),\mathsf{tail}_{A}(s))){\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{}\mathop{@}s}

Proof 9.3
.

We prove (2), the other two being similar. If
h:\mathsf{El}(A)
and
t:\mathsf{El}(\mathsf{Str}(A))
, note that
\mathsf{El}(\mathsf{Str}(A))
is a type of the form
\Gamma(-)
, and calculate that

\displaystyle\ \mathsf{tail}_{A}(\mathsf{cons}_{A}(h,t))

\displaystyle=\mathsf{tail}_{A}(\mathsf{cons}_{A}(h,\mathsf{mod}_{\gamma}(t^{\prime})))

write
t=\mathsf{mod}_{\gamma}(t^{\prime})
by modal induction

\displaystyle=\mathsf{tail}_{A}(\mathsf{mod}_{\gamma}((\mathsf{mod}_{\delta}(h),\mathsf{next}(t^{\prime}))))

\displaystyle=\mathbf{comp}_{\gamma,\ell}(\mathsf{mod}_{\gamma}(\mathsf{pr}_{1}((\mathsf{mod}_{\delta}(h),\mathsf{next}(t^{\prime})))))

\displaystyle=\mathbf{comp}_{\gamma,\ell}(\mathsf{mod}_{\gamma}(\mathsf{next}(t^{\prime})))

\displaystyle=\mathsf{mod}_{\gamma}(t^{\prime})

as
\gamma\circ\ell=\gamma

\displaystyle=t

Theorem 19
.

\mathsf{Str}(A)
is the final coalgebra for
\lambda X.\ \mathsf{El}(A)\times X:\mathsf{U}\to\mathsf{U}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{}\mathop{@}s}
.

Proof 9.4
.

Given
A:\mathsf{U}
we define a coalgebra
\mathsf{uncons}:\mathsf{Str}(A)\to(\mathsf{El}(A)\times\mathsf{Str}(A)){\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{}\mathop{@}s}
by

\mathsf{uncons}(s)\triangleq(\mathsf{head}_{A}(s),\mathsf{tail}_{A}(s))

To show finality, suppose
c:B\to\mathsf{El}(A)\times B{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{}\mathop{@}s}
is another
coalgebra. We define a function
f:B\to\mathsf{Str}(A){\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{}\mathop{@}s}
by

\begin{array}[]{lcl}f^{\prime}&:&\Delta B\to\mathsf{El}(\mathsf{Str}^{\prime}(A)){\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{}\mathop{@}t}\\
f^{\prime}&\triangleq&\textnormal{l{\"{o}}b}(\lambda f^{\prime\prime},x.\ \mathsf{let}\ \mathsf{mod}_{\delta}(x^{\prime})\leftarrow x\ \mathsf{in}\ (h,t))\\
&&\text{where }h=\mathsf{mod}_{\delta}(\mathsf{pr}_{0}(c(x^{\prime})))\\
&&\text{and}\quad\;\,t={f^{\prime\prime}\mathbin{\circledast_{\ell}}\mathsf{next}(\mathsf{mod}_{\delta}(\mathsf{pr}_{1}(c(x^{\prime}))))}\\[8.5359pt]
f&:&B\to\mathsf{El}(\mathsf{Str}(A)){\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{}\mathop{@}s}\\
f(x)&\triangleq&\mathsf{mod}_{\gamma}(f^{\prime}(\mathsf{mod}_{\delta}(x)))\end{array}

This is a morphism of coalgebras: for any
x:B
we have

\displaystyle\mathsf{uncons}(f(x))

\displaystyle=(\mathsf{head}_{A}(f(x)),\mathsf{tail}_{A}(f(x)))

\displaystyle=(\mathsf{pr}_{0}(c(x)),\mathsf{tail}_{A}(f(x)))

\displaystyle=(\mathsf{pr}_{0}(c(x)),\mathbf{comp}_{\gamma,\ell}(\mathsf{mod}_{\gamma}(\mathsf{pr}_{1}(f^{\prime}(x)))))

\displaystyle=(\mathsf{pr}_{0}(c(x)),\mathbf{comp}_{\gamma,\ell}(\mathsf{mod}_{\gamma}(\mathsf{next}(f^{\prime})\mathbin{\circledast_{\ell}}\mathsf{next}(\mathsf{mod}_{\delta}(\mathsf{pr}_{1}(c(x)))))))

\displaystyle=(\mathsf{pr}_{0}(c(x)),f(\mathsf{pr}_{1}(c(x))))

Finally, we must show that
f
is the unique coalgebra morphism. Suppose we are given

g:B\to\mathsf{El}(\mathsf{Str}(A)){\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{}\mathop{@}s}
which also satisfies

\mathsf{uncons}(g(x))=(\mathsf{pr}_{0}(c(x)),g(\mathsf{pr}_{1}(c(x))))
. We ‘shift’ this definition to timed sets, by defining

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\hat{g}&:&\Delta{B}\to\mathsf{El}(\mathsf{Str}^{\prime}(A)){\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{}\mathop{@}t}\\
\hat{g}(x)&\triangleq&\mathbf{coe}[\delta\circ\gamma\leq 1](\mathsf{mod}_{\delta}(g)\mathbin{\circledast_{\delta}}x)\end{array}

It suffices to show that
\hat{g}=f^{\prime}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{}\mathop{@}t}
, and we do so by Löb induction and
function extensionality. Assume
p:\RHD\mathsf{Eq}(\hat{g},f^{\prime})
, and
x:\Delta B
. To prove

\hat{g}(x)=f^{\prime}(x):\Delta B\times\RHD\mathsf{Str}^{\prime}(A)
it suffices to show componentwise equality.
By modal induction write
x=\mathsf{mod}_{\delta}(y)
for
y:B
.

First, we have that
\mathsf{pr}_{0}(f^{\prime}(\mathsf{mod}_{\delta}(y)))=\mathsf{mod}_{\delta}(\mathsf{pr}_{0}(c(y)))
by the definition of
f^{\prime}
. On the other hand, we have that

\mathsf{pr}_{0}(\hat{g}(\mathsf{mod}_{\delta}(y)))=\mathsf{pr}_{0}(\mathbf{coe}[\delta\circ\gamma\leq 1](\mathsf{mod}_{\delta}(g(y))))=\mathsf{pr}_{0}(g_{x}):\Delta B{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{}\mathop{@}t}

where we have used modal induction to write
g(y)=\mathsf{mod}_{\gamma}(g_{x})
. That
g
is a coalgebra
morphism implies that
\mathsf{head}(g(y))=\mathsf{pr}_{0}(c(y))
. If we now use modal induction to write

\mathsf{pr}_{0}(g_{x})=\mathsf{mod}_{\delta}(b)
for
b:B
and unfold the definition of
\mathsf{head}
, we obtain
b=\mathsf{pr}_{0}(c(y))
, so
\mathsf{pr}_{0}(g_{x})=\mathsf{mod}_{\delta}(b)=\mathsf{mod}_{\delta}(\mathsf{pr}_{0}(c(y)))
, which shows
that the two first components are equal.

For the second component, we compute that

\displaystyle\mathsf{pr}_{1}(f^{\prime}(\mathsf{mod}_{\delta}(y)))

\displaystyle=\mathsf{next}(f^{\prime})\mathbin{\circledast_{\ell}}\mathsf{next}(\mathsf{mod}_{\delta}(\mathsf{pr}_{1}(c(y)))))

\displaystyle=\mathsf{next}(f^{\prime}(\mathsf{mod}_{\delta}(\mathsf{pr}_{1}(c(y)))))

\displaystyle=\mathsf{next}(\hat{g}(\mathsf{mod}_{\delta}(\mathsf{pr}_{1}(c(y)))))

using
p
through Lemma
16

\displaystyle=\mathsf{next}(\mathbf{coe}[\delta\circ\gamma\leq 1](\mathsf{mod}_{\delta}(g(\mathsf{pr}_{1}(c(x))))))

\displaystyle=\mathsf{next}(\mathbf{coe}[\delta\circ\gamma\leq 1](\mathsf{mod}_{\delta}(\mathsf{tail}(g(y)))))

as
g
is a coalgebra morphism

\displaystyle=\mathsf{pr}_{1}(\mathbf{coe}[\delta\circ\gamma\leq 1](\mathsf{mod}_{\delta}(g(y))))

lemma

\displaystyle=\mathsf{pr}_{1}(\hat{g}(x))

The lemma referred to above is the fact that for any
s:\mathsf{Str}(A)
it is the case that

\mathsf{next}(\mathbf{coe}[\delta\circ\gamma\leq 1](\mathsf{mod}_{\delta}(\mathsf{tail}(s))))=\mathsf{pr}_{1}(\mathbf{coe}[\delta\circ\gamma\leq 1](\mathsf{mod}_{\delta}(s)))

which can be shown by a series of modal inductions.

We conclude this section by showing how to use these mechanisms in order to prove properties of
coinductive programs. Specifically, we will replicate a proof from
[
BGC+16
]
which shows
that the
zipWith
operator on streams preserves commutativity. Let

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\mathsf{zipWith}^{\prime}&:&\Delta(\mathsf{El}(A)\to\mathsf{El}(B)\to\mathsf{El}(C))\to\mathsf{El}(\mathsf{Str}^{\prime}(A))\to\mathsf{El}(\mathsf{Str}^{\prime}(B))\to\mathsf{El}(\mathsf{Str}^{\prime}(C))\\
\mathsf{zipWith}^{\prime}(f)&\triangleq&\textnormal{l{\"{o}}b}(\lambda r.\ \lambda x,y.\ (f\mathbin{\circledast_{\delta}}\mathsf{pr}_{0}(x)\mathbin{\circledast_{\delta}}\mathsf{pr}_{0}(y),r\mathbin{\circledast_{\ell}}\mathsf{pr}_{1}(x)\mathbin{\circledast_{\ell}}\mathsf{pr}_{1}(y)))\\[8.5359pt]
\mathsf{zipWith}&:&(\mathsf{El}(A)\to\mathsf{El}(B)\to\mathsf{El}(C))\to\mathsf{El}(\mathsf{Str}(A))\to\mathsf{El}(\mathsf{Str}(B))\to\mathsf{El}(\mathsf{Str}(C))\\
\mathsf{zipWith}(f)&\triangleq&\lambda x,y.\ \mathsf{mod}_{\gamma}(\mathsf{zipWith}^{\prime}(\mathsf{mod}_{\delta}(f)))\mathbin{\circledast_{\gamma}}x\mathbin{\circledast_{\gamma}}y\end{array}

Remark 20
.

Take note of a useful pattern for programming with guarded recursion, which is visible both here
and in the proof of Theorem
19
. We first define an auxiliary
function in mode
t
, which uses Löb induction. The main function itself is then just a thin
wrapper which ‘corrects’ that with the appropriate modalities and modal combinators.

Theorem 21
.

If
f
is commutative then
\mathsf{zipWith}(f)
is commutative. That is, given
A,B:\mathsf{U}
and

f:\mathsf{El}(A)\to\mathsf{El}(A)\to\mathsf{El}(B)
there is a term of the following type:

\displaystyle((x,y

\displaystyle:\mathsf{El}(A))\to\mathsf{Eq}_{\mathsf{El}(B)}(f(x,y),f(y,x)))\to{}

\displaystyle(s,t:\mathsf{El}(\mathsf{Str}(A)))\to\mathsf{Eq}_{\mathsf{El}(\mathsf{Str}(B))}(\mathsf{zipWith}(f,s,t),\mathsf{zipWith}(f,t,s))

Proof 9.5
.

Suppose
e:(x,y:\mathsf{El}(A))\to\mathsf{Eq}_{\mathsf{El}(B)}(f(x,y),f(y,x))
and
s,t:\mathsf{El}(\mathsf{Str}(A))
. We
wish to show that
\mathsf{zipWith}(f,s,t)=\mathsf{zipWith}(f,t,s)
. By the definition of
\mathsf{zipWith}
, it is sufficient to prove that for any
u,v:\mathsf{El}(\mathsf{Str}^{\prime}(A))
we have

\mathsf{zipWith}^{\prime}(\mathsf{mod}_{\delta}(f),u,v)=\mathsf{zipWith}^{\prime}(\mathsf{mod}_{\delta}(f),v,u)

In turn, it suffices to show that

\textnormal{l{\"{o}}b}(F_{0})=\textnormal{l{\"{o}}b}(F_{1})

where

\displaystyle F_{0}

\displaystyle\triangleq\lambda r.\ \lambda x,y.\ (\mathsf{mod}_{\delta}(f)\mathbin{\circledast_{\delta}}\mathsf{pr}_{0}(x)\mathbin{\circledast_{\delta}}\mathsf{pr}_{0}(y),r\mathbin{\circledast_{\ell}}\mathsf{pr}_{1}({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}x})\mathbin{\circledast_{\ell}}\mathsf{pr}_{1}({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}y}))

\displaystyle F_{1}

\displaystyle\triangleq\lambda r.\ \lambda x,y.\ (\mathsf{mod}_{\delta}(f)\mathbin{\circledast_{\delta}}\mathsf{pr}_{0}(y)\mathbin{\circledast_{\delta}}\mathsf{pr}_{0}(x),r\mathbin{\circledast_{\ell}}\mathsf{pr}_{1}({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}y})\mathbin{\circledast_{\ell}}\mathsf{pr}_{1}({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}x}))

because then

\mathsf{zipWith}^{\prime}(\mathsf{mod}_{\delta}(f),v,u)\triangleq\textnormal{l{\"{o}}b}(F_{0})(u,v)=\textnormal{l{\"{o}}b}(F_{1})(u,v)=\mathsf{zipWith}^{\prime}(\mathsf{mod}_{\delta}(f),u,v)

By Theorem
17
we know guarded fixed points are
unique, so it suffices to show that

\textnormal{l{\"{o}}b}(F_{1})=F_{0}(\mathsf{next}(\textnormal{l{\"{o}}b}(F_{1})))

(21)

We use Löb induction to construct a term of type
\mathsf{Eq}(\textnormal{l{\"{o}}b}(F_{1}),F_{0}(\mathsf{next}(\textnormal{l{\"{o}}b}(F_{1}))))
.

\displaystyle F_{0}(\mathsf{next}(\textnormal{l{\"{o}}b}(F_{1})))

\displaystyle=\lambda x,y.\ (\mathsf{mod}_{\delta}(f)\mathbin{\circledast_{\delta}}\mathsf{pr}_{0}(x)\mathbin{\circledast_{\delta}}\mathsf{pr}_{0}(y),\mathsf{next}(\textnormal{l{\"{o}}b}(F_{1}))\mathbin{\circledast_{\ell}}\mathsf{pr}_{1}(x)\mathbin{\circledast_{\ell}}\mathsf{pr}_{1}(y))

by induction let
\mathsf{mod}_{\delta}(a)\triangleq\mathsf{pr}_{0}(x)
and
\mathsf{mod}_{\delta}(b)\triangleq\mathsf{pr}_{0}(y)

\displaystyle=\lambda x,y.\ (\mathsf{mod}_{\delta}(f(a,b)),\mathsf{next}(\textnormal{l{\"{o}}b}(F_{1}))\mathbin{\circledast_{\ell}}\mathsf{pr}_{1}(x)\mathbin{\circledast_{\ell}}\mathsf{pr}_{1}(y))

\displaystyle=\lambda x,y.\ (\mathsf{mod}_{\delta}(f(b,a)),\mathsf{next}(\textnormal{l{\"{o}}b}(F_{1}))\mathbin{\circledast_{\ell}}\mathsf{pr}_{1}(x)\mathbin{\circledast_{\ell}}\mathsf{pr}_{1}(y))

\displaystyle=\lambda x,y.\ (\mathsf{mod}_{\delta}(f(b,a)),\mathsf{next}(F_{1}(\mathsf{next}(\textnormal{l{\"{o}}b}(F_{1}))))\mathbin{\circledast_{\ell}}\mathsf{pr}_{1}(x)\mathbin{\circledast_{\ell}}\mathsf{pr}_{1}(y))

by induction let
\mathsf{mod}_{\ell}(s)\triangleq\mathsf{pr}_{1}(x)
and
\mathsf{mod}_{\ell}(t)\triangleq\mathsf{pr}_{1}(y)

\displaystyle=\lambda x,y.\ (\mathsf{mod}_{\delta}(f(b,a)),\mathsf{next}(F_{1}(\mathsf{next}(\textnormal{l{\"{o}}b}(F_{1}))(s,t)))

\displaystyle=\lambda x,y.\ (\mathsf{mod}_{\delta}(f(b,a)),\mathsf{next}(F_{0}(\mathsf{next}(\textnormal{l{\"{o}}b}(F_{1}))(t,s)))

\displaystyle=\lambda x,y.\ (\mathsf{mod}_{\delta}(f(b,a)),\mathsf{next}(F_{0}(\mathsf{next}(\textnormal{l{\"{o}}b}(F_{1}))))\mathbin{\circledast_{\ell}}\mathsf{pr}_{1}(y)\mathbin{\circledast_{\ell}}\mathsf{pr}_{1}(x))

using the IH through Lemma
16

\displaystyle=\lambda x,y.\ (\mathsf{mod}_{\delta}(f(b,a)),\mathsf{next}(\textnormal{l{\"{o}}b}(F_{1}))\mathbin{\circledast_{\ell}}\mathsf{pr}_{1}(y)\mathbin{\circledast_{\ell}}\mathsf{pr}_{1}(x))

\displaystyle=\lambda x,y.\ (\mathsf{mod}_{\delta}(f)\mathbin{\circledast_{\delta}}\mathsf{pr}_{0}(y)\mathbin{\circledast_{\delta}}\mathsf{pr}_{0}(x),\mathsf{next}(\textnormal{l{\"{o}}b}(F_{1}))\mathbin{\circledast_{\ell}}\mathsf{pr}_{1}(y)\mathbin{\circledast_{\ell}}\mathsf{pr}_{1}(x))

\displaystyle=\textnormal{l{\"{o}}b}(F_{1})

Remark 22

(Previous approaches)
.

Using dependent type theories to reason about guarded recursion and coinductive types has been a
problem for some time
[
Møg14
]
. The technical device of
clocks
, due
to
[
AM13
]
, was introduced to deal with productivity in a simply-typed setting. Clocks
were then introduced to dependent types
[
Møg14
]
, and later refined into the
extensional guarded type theory
gDTT
of
[
BGC+16
]
.

In
gDTT
the problem of ‘totalising’ a type—which corresponds to reasoning by
coinduction—was not handled through the ‘always’ modality, but through clocks. In essence,

gDTT
does not come with a single
\RHD
modality, but rather with a collection of them,
each one indexed by a clock name. There is a quantifier which allows clock names to be bound
inside a particular type, and a crucial isomorphism:

\forall\kappa.\ A\cong\forall\kappa.\ \RHD^{\kappa}A

gDTT
presents several technical complications. The syntactic problems pertaining to
delayed substitutions were resolved by the introduction of Clocked Type Theory (CloTT)

[
BGM17
]
, which uses additional judgmental structure. It is conjectured that type-checking
is decidable for CloTT. The complexity of using clocks also appears in the
semantics of clocked type theory. CloTT is modelled in a collection of presheaf categories, with
multiple functors navigating between them
[
MM18
]
.

It was hoped that some of the complexity could be circumvented by replacing clocks with a
modality. This led Clouston et al. to introduce the comonadic ‘always’ modality
\Box
, which
replaced the isomorphism (
22
) with
\Box\RHD A\cong\Box A

[
CBGB15
]
. The main advantage of using
\Box
is that it can be interpreted in

\mathbf{PSh}(\omega)
, which is a much simpler model. On the other hand, the interactions between
\Box

and
\RHD
have proven difficult to capture in the syntax. In fact, the mere addition of
\Box

to a dependent type theory poses a significant technical challenge:
see
[
BGM17
,
BCM+20
,
Shu18
,
GSB19a
]
. Despite this concentrated
effort, there are still serious technical obstacles to adding
\RHD
to a type theory for

\Box
.
MTT
is the first syntax to accomodate both
\Box
,
\RHD
, and validate
\Box\RHD A\cong\Box A
.

10.
Internal Adjoints

In many cases of interest, the need for a pair of
adjoint modalities
arises: we would like a
pair of modalities
\mu:n\to m
and
\nu:m\to n
so that, in some sense,

\langle\nu\mid-\rangle\dashv\langle\mu\mid-\rangle

But what does it mean to have an adjunction between two modalities
within

MTT
? Does it
correspond to an external adjunction? And do all known results from category theory apply? The only
thing that is certain is that this scenario is fundamental to modal type theory, as a number of
intended models can be elegantly presented through
adjunctions
[
SS12
,
ND18
,
Shu18
]
.

In this section we show that when
MTT
is equipped with the
walking adjunction
as a mode
theory, it becomes a useful syntax for reasoning about adjoint modalities. Of course, the adjoint
modalities themselves are not exactly adjoint functors: they are something slightly weaker than
DRAs, whose ‘left adjoints’ constitute an adjunction. Nevertheless, we prove that the induced
modalities largely behave as expected: the unit and counit are internally definable; some limited
forms of internal transposition can be recovered; and left adjoints preserve colimits, as expressed
through
crisp induction principles
.

10.1.
The walking adjunction

As ever, we begin by freely defining a mode theory
\mathcal{M}_{\mathsf{adj}}
. Its generators are two 1-cells
\nu:m\to n
and
\mu:n\to m
, and two 2-cells

\displaystyle\eta

\displaystyle:1_{m}\Rightarrow\mu\circ\nu

\displaystyle\epsilon

\displaystyle:\nu\circ\mu\Rightarrow 1_{n}

subject to the triangle equations

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu\circ\nu\circ\mu}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta\mathop{\star}1_{\mu}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1_{\mu}\mathop{\star}\epsilon}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nu}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nu\circ\mu\circ\nu}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nu}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1_{\nu}\mathop{\star}\eta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\epsilon\mathop{\star}1_{\nu}}

\mathcal{M}_{\mathsf{adj}}
is sometimes called the
walking adjunction

[
LS16
, §5.1]
. It is the

classifying 2-category
for an adjunction: 2-functors
\mathcal{M}_{\mathsf{adj}}

\mathcal{C}
correspond
precisely to (2-categorical) adjunctions in
\mathcal{C}
. The mode theory
\mathcal{M}_{\mathsf{adj}}
has a very curious
property: it is
self-dual
, i.e. there is an equivalence
{\mathcal{M}_{\mathsf{adj}}}^{\mathsf{coop}}\simeq\mathcal{M}_{\mathsf{adj}}
.
This equivalence sends the modes to each other, the adjoints to themselves and the 2-cells
\eta

and
\epsilon
again to each other.

10.2.
Models of adjoint modalities

Recall that a modal context structure of a model of
MTT
with mode theory
\mathcal{M}_{\mathsf{adj}}
is a strict
2-functor
\llbracket-\rrbracket:{\mathcal{M}_{\mathsf{adj}}}^{\mathsf{coop}}\to\mathbf{Cat}
. The self-duality of
\mathcal{M}_{\mathsf{adj}}
implies that such
a context structure consists of two categories and an adjunction between them. We immediately obtain
the following result.

Corollary 23
.

If
\mathcal{C}
and
\mathcal{D}
carry models of
MLTT
, and there is a pair of dependent right adjoints
between them whose ‘left adjoints’ are themselves adjoint, then we can construct a model of
MTT

with mode theory
\mathcal{M}_{\mathsf{adj}}
.

Proof 10.1
.

Write
\llbracket\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{\nu}\rrbracket:\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{D}
and
\llbracket\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{\mu}\rrbracket:\mathcal{D}\to\mathcal{C}
for the functors
given as part of the DRAs. The notation is then suggestive:
\llbracket\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{\nu}\rrbracket\dashv\llbracket\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{\mu}\rrbracket
, and Theorem
8
applies.

Conversely,

Theorem 24
.

Any model of
\mathcal{M}_{\mathsf{adj}}
must interpret
\llbracket\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{\nu}\rrbracket
and
\llbracket\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{\mu}\rrbracket
as adjoint
functors. Moreover, if
\mathbf{Mod}_{\mu}
and
\mathbf{Mod}_{\nu}
are induced by lifting the adjunctions

\llbracket\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{\mu}\rrbracket\dashv R_{\mu}
and
\llbracket\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{\nu}\rrbracket\dashv R_{\nu}
to a dependent
right adjoints (by Lemma
10
), then
R_{\nu}\dashv R_{\mu}
.

Proof 10.2
.

Adjoint functors are precisely adjoint morphisms in the 2-category
\mathbf{Cat}
. As
\mathcal{M}_{\mathsf{adj}}
is the
walking adjunction, and 2-functors preserve adjunctions, we have that
\llbracket\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{\nu}\rrbracket\dashv\llbracket\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{\mu}\rrbracket
.

If
\llbracket\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{\nu}\rrbracket\dashv R_{\nu}
,
then by the uniqueness of adjoint pairs we must have that
R_{\nu}\cong\llbracket\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{\mu}\rrbracket
.
If moreover
\llbracket\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{\mu}\rrbracket\dashv R_{\mu}
,
then the previous isomorphism yields
R_{\nu}\dashv R_{\mu}
.

The last situation in this lemma is sometimes known as an ‘adjunction of adjunctions’
[
LS16
, §5.1]
. In particular, the action of the right adjoint modality
\mu

on contexts, viz.
\llbracket\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{\mu}\rrbracket
, is in some sense internalized on types and terms by the action of the left adjoint modality
\nu
on types and terms,
viz.
\langle\nu\mid-\rangle
.

10.3.
Recovering the adjunction internally

The foregoing construction of a model interpreted the lock functors required by
\mathcal{M}_{\mathsf{adj}}
by an
adjunction. Consequently, substitutions
\Delta\to\Gamma.\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{\mu}
are in natural bijection with
substitutions
\Delta.\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{\nu}\to\Gamma
. We would like to strengthen this setting by
bootstrapping this adjunction into an
internal adjunction
.

It is not immediately clear what an internal adjunction should be. However, we can construct an
appropriate definition by internalizing the unit and counit as functions. But that is not immediate
either: if
\Gamma\vdash A\ \mathsf{type}_{1}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{}\mathop{@}{m}}
, the construction
\langle\mu\mid\langle\nu\mid A\rangle\rangle
that we would naïvely
try as the codomain of the unit is ill-typed. This can be mended through key substitutions. Recall
that
\eta:1_{m}\Rightarrow\mu\circ\nu
. The corresponding key substitution at
\Gamma{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{}\mathop{@}m}
is
\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.85}{$\displaystyle\text{\faIconFromMacro{faKey}}$}}{\scalebox{0.85}{$\textstyle\text{\faIconFromMacro{faKey}}$}}{\scalebox{0.85}{$\scriptstyle\text{\faIconFromMacro{faKey}}$}}{\scalebox{0.85}{$\scriptscriptstyle\text{\faIconFromMacro{faKey}}$}}_{\Gamma}^{\eta}:\Gamma,\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{\mu\circ\nu}\to\Gamma{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{}\mathop{@}m}
. We can use this to formally define the notation of
Section
2.3
by

A^{\eta}\triangleq A[\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.85}{$\displaystyle\text{\faIconFromMacro{faKey}}$}}{\scalebox{0.85}{$\textstyle\text{\faIconFromMacro{faKey}}$}}{\scalebox{0.85}{$\scriptstyle\text{\faIconFromMacro{faKey}}$}}{\scalebox{0.85}{$\scriptscriptstyle\text{\faIconFromMacro{faKey}}$}}_{\Gamma}^{\eta}]

As substitutions can be eliminated (e.g. through a subset of the canonicity algorithm), this defines
an admissible operation from type
\Gamma\vdash A\ \mathsf{type}_{1}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{}\mathop{@}{m}}
to type
\Gamma,\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{\mu\circ\nu}\vdash A^{\eta}\ \mathsf{type}_{1}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{}\mathop{@}{m}}
. We can thus define the unit component at
\Gamma\vdash A\ \mathsf{type}_{1}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{}\mathop{@}{m}}
by

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\mathsf{unit}&:&A\to\langle\mu\mid\langle\nu\mid A^{\eta}\rangle\rangle{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{}\mathop{@}m}\\
\mathsf{unit}(x)&\triangleq&\mathsf{mod}_{\mu}(\mathsf{mod}_{\nu}(x^{\eta}))\end{array}

Dually, for any type
\Gamma,\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{\nu\circ\mu}\vdash A\ \mathsf{type}_{1}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{}\mathop{@}{n}}
we can define the counit
component by

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\mathsf{counit}&:&\langle\nu\mid\langle\mu\mid A\rangle\rangle\to A^{\epsilon}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{}\mathop{@}n}\\
\mathsf{counit}(x)&\triangleq&\mathsf{let}\ \mathsf{mod}_{\nu}(y_{0})\leftarrow x\ \mathsf{in}\ \mathsf{let}_{\nu}\ \mathsf{mod}_{\mu}(y_{1})\leftarrow y_{0}\ \mathsf{in}\ y_{1}^{\epsilon}\end{array}

We thus obtain the unit and counit internally, but the types of the components have to be adjusted
in the presence of dependence. Moreover, we can prove internal versions of the triangle equations;
they are given by modal induction:

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\_&:&(x:\langle\nu\mid A\rangle)\to\mathsf{Id}_{\langle\nu\mid A\rangle}(x,\mathsf{counit}(\mathsf{mod}_{\nu}(\mathsf{unit})\mathbin{\circledast_{\nu}}x))\\
\_&\triangleq&\lambda x.\ \mathsf{let}\ \mathsf{mod}_{\nu}(y)\leftarrow x\ \mathsf{in}\ \mathsf{refl}(\mathsf{mod}_{\nu}(y))\\[8.5359pt]
\_&:&(x:\langle\mu\mid A\rangle)\to\mathsf{Id}_{\langle\mu\mid A\rangle}(x,\mathsf{mod}_{\mu}(\mathsf{counit})\mathbin{\circledast_{\mu}}\mathsf{unit}(x))\\
\_&\triangleq&\lambda x.\ \mathsf{let}\ \mathsf{mod}_{\mu}(y)\leftarrow x\ \mathsf{in}\ \mathsf{refl}(\mathsf{mod}_{\mu}(y))\end{array}

The most difficult part is proving that these terms are well-typed. For example, in the first
instance we must show that
\mathsf{mod}_{\nu}(y)=\mathsf{counit}(\mathsf{mod}_{\nu}(\mathsf{unit})\mathbin{\circledast_{\nu}}\mathsf{mod}_{\nu}(y))
definitionally:

\displaystyle\mathsf{counit}(\mathsf{mod}_{\nu}(\mathsf{unit})\mathbin{\circledast_{\nu}}\mathsf{mod}_{\nu}(y))

\displaystyle=\mathsf{counit}(\mathsf{mod}_{\nu}(\mathsf{unit}(y)))

\displaystyle=\mathsf{counit}(\mathsf{mod}_{\nu}(\mathsf{mod}_{\mu}(\mathsf{mod}_{\nu}(y^{\eta}))))

\displaystyle=\mathsf{mod}_{\nu}(y^{\eta})^{\epsilon}

\displaystyle=\mathsf{mod}_{\nu}((y^{\eta})^{\epsilon\mathop{\star}1_{\nu}})

\displaystyle=\mathsf{mod}_{\nu}(y^{(\epsilon\mathop{\star}1_{\nu})\circ(1_{\nu}\mathop{\star}\eta)})

\displaystyle=\mathsf{mod}_{\nu}(y)

Because we are using slightly informal syntax here, it is difficult to see that the steps that
introduce whiskering are correct. They become much more perspucious if we expand

\mathsf{mod}_{\nu}(y^{\eta})^{\epsilon}
into algebraic syntax, and use the last equation of
Fig.
9
twice to absorb locks:

\mathsf{mod}_{\nu}(y[\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.85}{$\displaystyle\text{\faIconFromMacro{faKey}}$}}{\scalebox{0.85}{$\textstyle\text{\faIconFromMacro{faKey}}$}}{\scalebox{0.85}{$\scriptstyle\text{\faIconFromMacro{faKey}}$}}{\scalebox{0.85}{$\scriptscriptstyle\text{\faIconFromMacro{faKey}}$}}_{\Gamma.\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{\nu}}^{\eta}])[\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.85}{$\displaystyle\text{\faIconFromMacro{faKey}}$}}{\scalebox{0.85}{$\textstyle\text{\faIconFromMacro{faKey}}$}}{\scalebox{0.85}{$\scriptstyle\text{\faIconFromMacro{faKey}}$}}{\scalebox{0.85}{$\scriptscriptstyle\text{\faIconFromMacro{faKey}}$}}_{\Gamma}^{\epsilon}]=\mathsf{mod}_{\nu}(y[\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.85}{$\displaystyle\text{\faIconFromMacro{faKey}}$}}{\scalebox{0.85}{$\textstyle\text{\faIconFromMacro{faKey}}$}}{\scalebox{0.85}{$\scriptstyle\text{\faIconFromMacro{faKey}}$}}{\scalebox{0.85}{$\scriptscriptstyle\text{\faIconFromMacro{faKey}}$}}_{\Gamma.\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{\nu}}^{\eta}\circ\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.85}{$\displaystyle\text{\faIconFromMacro{faKey}}$}}{\scalebox{0.85}{$\textstyle\text{\faIconFromMacro{faKey}}$}}{\scalebox{0.85}{$\scriptstyle\text{\faIconFromMacro{faKey}}$}}{\scalebox{0.85}{$\scriptscriptstyle\text{\faIconFromMacro{faKey}}$}}_{\Gamma}^{\epsilon}.\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{\nu}])=\mathsf{mod}_{\nu}(y[\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.85}{$\displaystyle\text{\faIconFromMacro{faKey}}$}}{\scalebox{0.85}{$\textstyle\text{\faIconFromMacro{faKey}}$}}{\scalebox{0.85}{$\scriptstyle\text{\faIconFromMacro{faKey}}$}}{\scalebox{0.85}{$\scriptscriptstyle\text{\faIconFromMacro{faKey}}$}}_{\Gamma}^{1_{\nu}\mathop{\star}\eta}\circ\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.85}{$\displaystyle\text{\faIconFromMacro{faKey}}$}}{\scalebox{0.85}{$\textstyle\text{\faIconFromMacro{faKey}}$}}{\scalebox{0.85}{$\scriptstyle\text{\faIconFromMacro{faKey}}$}}{\scalebox{0.85}{$\scriptscriptstyle\text{\faIconFromMacro{faKey}}$}}_{\Gamma}^{\epsilon\mathop{\star}1_{\nu}}])=\mathsf{mod}_{\nu}(y[\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.85}{$\displaystyle\text{\faIconFromMacro{faKey}}$}}{\scalebox{0.85}{$\textstyle\text{\faIconFromMacro{faKey}}$}}{\scalebox{0.85}{$\scriptstyle\text{\faIconFromMacro{faKey}}$}}{\scalebox{0.85}{$\scriptscriptstyle\text{\faIconFromMacro{faKey}}$}}_{\Gamma}^{1_{\mu}}])

10.4.
Internal transposition

The previous section offers a perfectly good internal representation of an external adjunction.
However, it is usually much more economical to present an adjunction by a natural isomorphism

\mathrm{Hom}(L(A),B)\cong\mathrm{Hom}(A,R(B))

Unfortunately, this is not achievable in
MTT
for a multitude of reasons. First, notice that

\langle\nu\mid A\rangle\to B{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{}\mathop{@}n}
and
A\to\langle\mu\mid B\rangle{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{}\mathop{@}m}
are
types in different modes, so the putative type
(\langle\nu\mid A\rangle\to B)\simeq(A\to\langle\mu\mid B\rangle)

that would represent the isomorphism is ill-typed.

Second, even if the two modes coincide—so that
\nu,\mu
are endomodalities—the aforementioned
type is a bit too strong for our purpose: it is inhabited by
internal equivalences
, which are
stronger than bijections of hom-sets. Such equivalences correspond to isomorphisms
B^{L(A)}\cong R(B)^{A}
of exponential objects. In turn, these are equivalent to hom-set bijections only if the
involved functors are
internal
, which is to say that we have functions

\displaystyle(A\to B)

\displaystyle\to(\langle\nu\mid A\rangle\to\langle\nu\mid B\rangle)

\displaystyle(A\to B)

\displaystyle\to(\langle\mu\mid A\rangle\to\langle\mu\mid B\rangle)

that compute the action of the modality
\langle\mu\mid-\rangle
on morphisms
within

MTT
.
10
10

10

Such functors are usually called
enriched
(recall that cartesian closure is
a self-enrichment).

Third, even if we could internalize our modalities, we would be flying too close to the sun. As we
have
(1\to A)\simeq A
for any
A
and
\langle\xi\mid 1\rangle\simeq 1
for any
\xi
, we may calculate
that

A\ \simeq\ 1\to A\ \simeq\ \langle\nu\mid 1\rangle\to A\ \simeq\ 1\to\langle\mu\mid A\rangle\ \simeq\ \langle\mu\mid A\rangle

Hence,
\langle\mu\mid-\rangle
must be the identity functor up to equivalence. This short argument, which is
due to
[
LOPS18
, Theorem 5.1]
, is a
no-go theorem
that obstructs the
internalization of an adjunction whose left adjoint preserves terminal objects.

[
LOPS18
]
overcame this barrier by introducing the
global sections
modality

\flat
. Terms of
\flat A
represent
global
elements of
A
: terms of
\flat(A\to B)
are in bijection with morphisms in
\mathrm{Hom}(A,B)
. Thus, the previously problematic equivalence
holds under
\flat
.

We can rephrase this argument in our syntax. The key thing to notice is that the functor
\flat:\mathbf{PSh}(\mathcal{C})\to\mathbf{PSh}(\mathcal{C})
which maps a presheaf to the constant presheaf
\_\mapsto\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbf{PSh}(\mathcal{C})}(1,P)
of global sections is initial amongst functors that preserve the terminal
object. Thus, we postulate an initial modality: suppose that
n=m
, and that
\mathrm{Hom}(m,m)
is
equipped with an initial object, i.e. a 1-cell
\flat:m\to m
and a unique 2-cell
{!}:\flat\Rightarrow\xi
for all
\xi
. As a consequence, we are able to use variables
x:(\flat\mid A)
in any
context. Assuming function extensionality, we have that

Theorem 25
.

There is an equivalence
\langle\flat\mid\langle\nu\mid A^{!}\rangle\to B\rangle\simeq\langle\flat\mid A\to\langle\mu\mid B^{!}\rangle\rangle
.

Proof 10.3
.

The equivalence is given by the functions

\begin{array}[]{lclclcl}F&:&\langle\flat\mid\langle\nu\mid A^{!}\rangle\to B\rangle\to\langle\flat\mid A\to\langle\mu\mid B^{!}\rangle\rangle\\
F(f)&\triangleq&\mathsf{let}\ \mathsf{mod}_{\flat}(g)\leftarrow f\ \mathsf{in}\ \mathsf{mod}_{\flat}(\lambda x.\ \mathsf{mod}_{\mu}(g^{!})\mathbin{\circledast_{\mu}}\mathsf{unit}(x))\\[8.5359pt]
G&:&\langle\flat\mid A\to\langle\mu\mid B^{!}\rangle\rangle\to\langle\nu\mid A\rangle\to\langle\flat\mid\langle\nu\mid A^{!}\rangle\to B\rangle\\
G(g)&\triangleq&\mathsf{let}\ \mathsf{mod}_{\flat}(f)\leftarrow g\ \mathsf{in}\ \mathsf{mod}_{\flat}(\lambda x.\ \mathsf{counit}(\mathsf{mod}_{\nu}(f^{!})\mathbin{\circledast_{\nu}}x))\end{array}

These are well-typed because, by initiality of
\flat
,
A^{\eta}=(A^{!})^{\eta}=A^{!}
,

(B^{!})^{\epsilon}=B^{!}
. By function extensionality and
\eta
for modalities, they are mutually
inverse.

The closest we can get to defining internal transposition (without using an initial modality)
amounts to the following two functions.

\begin{array}[]{lclclcl}\mathbf{transp}_{\nu\dashv\mu}^{\rightarrow}&:&\langle\mu\mid\langle\nu\mid A^{\eta}\rangle\to B\rangle\to A\to\langle\mu\mid B\rangle\\
\mathbf{transp}_{\nu\dashv\mu}^{\rightarrow}&\triangleq&\lambda f.\ \lambda x.\ f\mathbin{\circledast_{\mu}}\mathsf{unit}(x)\\[8.5359pt]
\mathbf{transp}_{\nu\dashv\mu}^{\leftarrow}&:&\langle\nu\mid A\to\langle\mu\mid B\rangle\rangle\to\langle\nu\mid A\rangle\to B^{\epsilon}\\
\mathbf{transp}_{\nu\dashv\mu}^{\leftarrow}&\triangleq&\lambda f.\ \lambda x.\ \mathsf{counit}(f\mathbin{\circledast_{\nu}}x)\end{array}

The first is an equivalence (again up to function extensionality), but neither have the expected
type. The first transposition
\mathbf{transp}_{\nu\dashv\mu}^{\rightarrow}
is not without precedent: it is the internal
formulation of transposition for adjunctions between monoidal closed categories when the left
adjoint preserves monoidal products.

10.5.
Crisp induction

Having internalized the definition of an adjunction, it is natural to ask whether standard facts
about adjoint functors carry over. In this section we prove an internal version of the fact that
left adjoints preserve colimits. Within type theory this result takes the form of
crisp
induction principles
for various types that arise from colimits.

As a first approximation to the notion of crisp induction, recall the rule for modal induction,
i.e. the elimination rule for modal types from Section
2
:

\frac{\mu:n\to m\quad\nu:m\to o\quad\Gamma,x:({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\nu}\mid\langle\mu\mid A\rangle)\vdash B\ \mathsf{type}_{1}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{}\mathop{@}{o}}\quad\quad\Gamma,\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\nu}}\vdash M_{0}:\langle\mu\mid A\rangle{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{}\mathop{@}m}\quad\Gamma,x:({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\nu}\circ\mu\mid A)\vdash M_{1}:B[\mathsf{mod}_{\mu}(x)/x]{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{}\mathop{@}o}}{\Gamma\vdash\mathsf{let}_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\nu}}\ \mathsf{mod}_{\mu}(x)\leftarrow M_{0}\ \mathsf{in}\ M_{1}:B[M_{0}/x]{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{}\mathop{@}o}}

Notice that there is an “extra” modality parameterizing this rule,
\nu
, which modifies
M_{0}
as
well as the data supplied to
M_{1}
. This extra generality is not frivolous: we can only define the
equivalence
\mathbf{comp}_{\mu,\nu}
of Section
3
because we can eliminate one
modality ‘under’ another.

One might hope for a similar level of flexibility in all positive eliminators. However, the
elimination rule for booleans—stated here in its algebraic form of
Section
4
—does not allow it:

\frac{\Gamma\ \mathsf{ctx}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{}\mathop{@}m}\quad\Gamma.({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}\mid\mathbb{B})\vdash A\ \mathsf{type}_{1}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{}\mathop{@}{m}}\quad\Gamma\vdash M_{t}:A[{\mathsf{id}}.{\mathsf{tt}}]{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{}\mathop{@}m}\quad\Gamma\vdash M_{f}:A[{\mathsf{id}}.{\mathsf{ff}}]{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{}\mathop{@}m}\quad\Gamma.\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}}\vdash N:\mathbb{B}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{}\mathop{@}m}}{\Gamma\vdash\mathsf{if}(A;M_{t};M_{f};N):A[{\mathsf{id}}.{N}]{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{}\mathop{@}m}}

Were we to replace
1
with an arbitrary modality, then this rule would state something considerably
stronger: not only would we have the expected elimination principle for
\mathbb{B}
, but all of our
modalities would
preserve

\mathbb{B}
. Semantically, this is nonsense: modalities intuitively
correspond to right adjoints, and therefore do not necessarily preserve colimits. For example, the
later
\RHD
modality of Section
9
does not preserve booleans.

Yet, in some circumstances—e.g. when a modality is a left adjoint—the stronger rule is valid.
This is the idea behind Shulman’s crisp induction principles
[
Shu18
, §5]
: cohesive
type theory enables the proof of elimination principles for the coproducts and the identity type
under the left adjoint in the adjunction
\flat\dashv\sharp
. We will demonstrate that similar
principles are derivable within
MTT
with mode theory
\mathcal{M}_{\mathsf{adj}}
.

Fix a motive
\Gamma,\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{\nu\circ\mu},b:(\nu\mid\mathbb{B})\vdash C\ \mathsf{type}_{1}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{}\mathop{@}{n}}
. Crisp
induction is given by a term

\Gamma\vdash{}\mathsf{crisp\_if}_{C}:(b:(\nu\mid\mathbb{B}))\to\langle\nu\circ\mu\mid C(\mathsf{tt})\rangle\to\langle\nu\circ\mu\mid C(\mathsf{ff})\rangle\to C^{\epsilon}(b){\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{}\mathop{@}n}

This is a well-formed type, as
\Gamma,b:(\nu\mid\mathbb{B})=\Gamma,\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{1},b:(\nu\mid\mathbb{B})\vdash C^{\epsilon}\ \mathsf{type}_{1}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{}\mathop{@}{m}}
.

To obtain the crisp induction principle we first use the ordinary one at mode
n
, and apply a
number of modal combinators to bring it to mode
m
.

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\Gamma,\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{\nu}\vdash{}h&:&(b:\mathbb{B})\to\langle\mu\mid C(\mathsf{tt})\rangle\to\langle\mu\mid C(\mathsf{ff})\rangle\to\langle\mu\mid C(b^{\eta})\rangle{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{}\mathop{@}m}\\
h(b,t,f)&\triangleq&\mathsf{if}(b.\ \langle\mu\mid C(b^{\eta})\rangle;t;f;b)\\[8.5359pt]
\Gamma\vdash{}\mathsf{crisp\_if}_{C}&:&(b:(\nu\mid\mathbb{B}))\to\langle\nu\circ\mu\mid C(\mathsf{tt})\rangle\to\langle\nu\circ\mu\mid C(\mathsf{ff})\rangle\to C^{\epsilon}(b){\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{}\mathop{@}m}\\
\mathsf{crisp\_if}_{C}(b,t,f)&\triangleq&\mathsf{counit}(\mathsf{mod}_{\nu}(h(b))\mathbin{\circledast_{\nu}}\mathbf{comp}_{\nu,\mu}^{\text{-1}}(t)\mathbin{\circledast_{\nu}}\mathbf{comp}_{\nu,\mu}^{\text{-1}}(f))\end{array}

The reasons why this term is well-typed is subtle. We have that

\Gamma,\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{\nu},b:(1\mid\mathbb{B}),\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{\mu\circ\nu}\vdash b^{\eta}:\mathbb{B}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{}\mathop{@}m}
, so

\Gamma,\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{\nu},b:(1\mid\mathbb{B}),\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{\mu}\vdash C(b^{\eta})\ \mathsf{type}_{1}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{}\mathop{@}{n}}
by the
application rule. Thus,
h
is well-typed. It remains to show that
C(b^{\eta})^{\epsilon}=C^{\epsilon}(b)
, which intuitively follows from the triangle identities. We may show it by precisely
specifying what these operations mean in the algebraic syntax. First, we construct the substitutions

\displaystyle\sigma_{0}

\displaystyle\triangleq{{\uparrow}.\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{\mu}}.{\mathbf{v}_{0}[\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.85}{$\displaystyle\text{\faIconFromMacro{faKey}}$}}{\scalebox{0.85}{$\textstyle\text{\faIconFromMacro{faKey}}$}}{\scalebox{0.85}{$\scriptstyle\text{\faIconFromMacro{faKey}}$}}{\scalebox{0.85}{$\scriptscriptstyle\text{\faIconFromMacro{faKey}}$}}_{\Gamma.\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{\nu}.(1\mid\mathbb{B})}^{\eta}]}\

\displaystyle:\Gamma.\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{\nu}.(1\mid\mathbb{B}).\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{\mu}\to\Gamma.\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{\nu\circ\mu}.(\nu\mid\mathbb{B}){\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{}\mathop{@}n}

\displaystyle\sigma_{1}

\displaystyle\triangleq{{\uparrow}.\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{\nu}}.{\mathbf{v}_{0}}.\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{\mu}\

\displaystyle:\Gamma.(\nu\mid\mathbb{B}).\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{\nu\circ\mu}\to\Gamma.\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{\nu}.(1\mid\mathbb{B}).\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{\mu}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{}\mathop{@}n}

\displaystyle\sigma_{2}

\displaystyle\triangleq{\left(\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.85}{$\displaystyle\text{\faIconFromMacro{faKey}}$}}{\scalebox{0.85}{$\textstyle\text{\faIconFromMacro{faKey}}$}}{\scalebox{0.85}{$\scriptstyle\text{\faIconFromMacro{faKey}}$}}{\scalebox{0.85}{$\scriptscriptstyle\text{\faIconFromMacro{faKey}}$}}_{\Gamma}^{\epsilon}\circ{\uparrow}\right)}.{\mathbf{v}_{0}}\

\displaystyle:\Gamma.(\nu\mid\mathbb{B})\to\Gamma.\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{\nu\circ\mu}.(\nu\mid\mathbb{B}){\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{}\mathop{@}n}

We can then interpret
C(b^{\eta})
as the type

\Gamma.\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{\nu}.(1\mid\mathbb{B}).\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{\mu}\vdash C[\sigma_{0}]\ \mathsf{type}_{1}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{}\mathop{@}{n}}
. Similarly,

C(b^{\eta})^{\epsilon}
is the type

\Gamma.(\nu\mid\mathbb{B})\vdash C[\sigma_{0}][\sigma_{1}][\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.85}{$\displaystyle\text{\faIconFromMacro{faKey}}$}}{\scalebox{0.85}{$\textstyle\text{\faIconFromMacro{faKey}}$}}{\scalebox{0.85}{$\scriptstyle\text{\faIconFromMacro{faKey}}$}}{\scalebox{0.85}{$\scriptscriptstyle\text{\faIconFromMacro{faKey}}$}}_{\Gamma.(\nu\mid\mathbb{B})}^{\epsilon}]\ \mathsf{type}_{1}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{}\mathop{@}{n}}
.
Finally,
C^{\epsilon}(b)
is the type
\Gamma.(\nu\mid\mathbb{B})\vdash C[\sigma_{2}]\ \mathsf{type}_{1}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{}\mathop{@}{n}}
, so
it suffices to show that
